An unnamed young girl whose lover was unfaithful, dismissed at once; Niobe; Antigone; Job and his friends; the father of the prodigal son; an unnamed pale figure, a youth who had sought martyrdom; a second young girl whose lover was unfaithful, who is awarded the *accessit*; and finally the unhappiest one himself, unnamed.

### Q: A parades eight figures at the end of "The Unhappiest One". List the last four in order.
**Topic:** The gallery in The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 31d0dd60
**A:** Parts:
1. The father of the prodigal son.
2. An unnamed pale figure, a youth who had sought martyrdom.
3. A second young girl whose lover was unfaithful.
4. The unhappiest one himself, unnamed.

The first four are an unnamed young girl whose lover was unfaithful, Niobe, Antigone, and Job and his friends. The second young girl is the one awarded the *accessit*, the runner-up prize.

### Q: Why is the first young girl dismissed in three lines, and what does that tell you about A's test?
**Topic:** The gallery in The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** derive  **Id:** 6108e8d9
**A:** Key:
1. She loved him with her whole soul, so she can remember and grieve.
2. Grieving means being present in your memory, so she is not unhappy in the strict sense.
3. The test is not how much was lost but whether either direction in time still holds a present.

Because she loved him with her whole soul and so she can remember and grieve. Grieving means being present in your memory, and whoever is present in his memory is not unhappy in the strict sense. The test is not how much was lost but whether either direction in time still holds a present for you. That is why the second girl, who can neither grieve nor hope, gets much further.

### Q: Who is Niobe, what does A say her position is, and why does she not win?
**Topic:** The gallery in The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 0fc4a5a9
**A:** Parts:
1. In the myth Niobe boasted of being Leto's equal, Apollo and Artemis killed her twelve children, and Zeus turned her to stone.
2. A says she lost everything at a single blow and stands hopeless, petrified in memory.
3. She does not win because she was unhappy for one instant and in that same instant became happy.
4. Her memory is of something real.

In the myth she boasted of being Leto's equal, Apollo and Artemis killed all twelve of her children, and Zeus turned her to stone. A asks whether she is a real being or an image, a living person dying or a dead one living. She lost everything at a single blow: she lost what she had given life to, and she lost what gave her life. No hope beckons her; she stands hopeless, petrified in memory. She does not win because she was unhappy for one instant and in that same instant became happy, and nothing can take that from her. Her memory is of something real.

### Q: What does A say about Antigone, and what does he wish her?
**Topic:** The gallery in The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 2e16c770
**A:** Parts:
1. Antigone is the last of the outcast house of Oedipus, crushed by a blow passing from one generation to the next.
2. She has turned her back on hope and exchanged its inconstancy for the loyalty of memory.
3. A wishes her a long life as full of meaning as a deep sigh.
4. He wishes that no forgetfulness take anything from her and that grief's daily bitterness be given her in abundance.

She is the last of the outcast house of Oedipus, the blow passing from one generation to the next until it crushes her. A says one generation's sorrow is enough for one human life, and that she has turned her back on hope and exchanged its inconstancy for the loyalty of memory. He then wishes her a long life as full of meaning as a deep sigh, that no forgetfulness take anything from her, and that the daily bitterness of grief be given to her in abundance.

### Q: What is A's line about Job, and on what ground does he refuse him the grave?
**Topic:** The gallery in The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** ee4e6e68
**A:** Parts:
1. The line is "He lost everything, but not at one blow; for the Lord took, and the Lord took, and the Lord took."
2. A refuses him the grave because he had possessed what he lost.

"He lost everything, but not at one blow; for the Lord took, and the Lord took, and the Lord took." His friends taught him to feel the bitterness of the loss, since the Lord gave and gave and gave, and threw in a foolish wife as well. A honours him for his grey hair and his misfortune and then refuses him in one clause: he lost everything, but he had possessed it. Job's memory is of a real past, so he is present in it.

### Q: Who is the only hoper in the gallery, and how does A contrast him with the figure before him?
**Topic:** The gallery in The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** apply  **Id:** a43918b8
**A:** Key:
1. The only hoper is the father of the prodigal son.
2. Job's loss was taken by the Lord, whereas here the enemy took it.
3. Job has lost; this man is losing, because it was not taken from him but is vanishing.
4. Unlike Job in sackcloth and ashes at home, he has left home to search.

The father of the prodigal son. The contrast with Job is exact and A draws it himself: Job lost what was dearest and the Lord took it, whereas here the enemy took it; Job has lost, this man is losing; it was not taken from him, it is vanishing. He does not sit at home in sackcloth and ashes like Job but has left home to search. His hope makes him old, and A repeats the refrain as the body fails: his feet are tired, his eyes dim, his body seeks rest, his hope lives; his hair is white, his foot halts, his heart breaks, his hope lives.

### Q: Describe the pale figure in the gallery, say who he is commonly but wrongly taken to be, and say why that is wrong.
**Topic:** The gallery in The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 68c3ae22
**A:** Parts:
1. He is a youth whose name is forgotten, who sought martyrdom but denied his Master and himself when reality proved too heavy.
2. He became a martyr after all, not by the cross or the beasts but by being burned alive and slowly consumed.
3. He is commonly but wrongly taken for the apostle Peter.
4. He is not Peter: his name is forgotten, which Peter's is not, and Peter was crucified rather than burned.

A youth whose name has been forgotten, many centuries having passed. He sought martyrdom and saw himself in imagination nailed to the cross with heaven open, but reality proved too heavy, the fervour disappeared, and he denied his Master and himself. He was not crushed but broken, his spirit enfeebled. And he did become a martyr after all, though not by the cross or the beasts but by being burned alive and slowly consumed. He is often taken for the apostle Peter, because of the denial, but he is not: the text says his name is forgotten, which is exactly what Peter's is not, and Peter was crucified rather than burned. The wording borrows Peter's denial for a generic lapsed would-be martyr.

### Q: Why does the second young girl come closest without winning, what is she awarded, and what is that award?
**Topic:** The gallery in The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** bf3b0645
**A:** Parts:
1. She can neither grieve, since perhaps he was not a deceiver, nor hope, since he was a riddle.
2. She is awarded an honourable *accessit* and the place next to the grave, but not the grave.
3. An *accessit* is the runner-up prize for the second-best answer in a university prize competition.

Because she can do neither of the two things that would keep her present to herself. She cannot grieve, since perhaps he was not unfaithful and not a deceiver; and she cannot hope, since he was a riddle. A tells her she must divide herself and hope by day and grieve by night, or grieve by day and hope by night, and that she should be proud, because one should never be proud of happiness but may be of unhappiness. She is awarded an honourable *accessit* and the place next to the grave, but not the grave. An *accessit* is the runner-up prize given for the second-best answer in a university prize competition.

### Q: What titles does A heap on the unhappiest one when he finally appears, and what does A call him by name?
**Topic:** The gallery in The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 53c07c91
**A:** Parts:
1. A calls him the emissary from the kingdom of sighs, the elected favourite of suffering and the apostle of sorrow.
2. A also calls him the silent friend of pain and the unhappy lover of memory.
3. He has no name: A greets him as the great unknown whose name he does not know.

The emissary from the kingdom of sighs, the elected favourite of suffering, the apostle of sorrow, the silent friend of pain, and the unhappy lover of memory — confounded in his memory by the light of hope, and deceived in his hope by the shadows of memory. He has no name. A greets him as the great unknown whose name he does not know, and salutes him with his title of honour.

### Q: What is the real-world source of the grave in "The Unhappiest One", and what does Kierkegaard's own note about it say?
**Topic:** The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 7bf05552
**A:** Parts:
1. The source is a grave slab in Worcester Cathedral inscribed *Miserrimus*, Latin for "the most wretched".
2. The note says a reader might conclude nobody lay buried there, but that the grave was meant for him.

A grave slab in Worcester Cathedral inscribed *Miserrimus*, Latin for "the most wretched". Kierkegaard recorded it in a notebook in 1840, and the note already contains the whole conceit: that someone might read the inscription and conclude that nobody at all lay buried there, but that the grave was meant for him.

### Q: Name the two Easter allusions in "The Unhappiest One" and say what they do to the essay.
**Topic:** The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** derive  **Id:** e7608631
**A:** Parts:
1. The first allusion is the empty grave at the opening.
2. The second is "the stone is rolled away" near the end.
3. They make the grave an inverted Holy Sepulchre: Christ's is empty because he rose, this one because its man has never lived.

The empty grave at the opening, and "the stone is rolled away" when A greets the winner near the end. Both echo the women at Christ's tomb in Mark 16. They make the grave in the unhappy west an inverted Holy Sepulchre: Christ's tomb is empty because he rose to life, and this one is empty because the man who belongs in it has not been found and has never lived.

### Q: A offers the unhappiest one two opposite wishes. Give both, and say why he offers the second.
**Topic:** The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 1447dfa4
**A:** Parts:
1. First wish: that no one understand him but everyone envy him, with no friend, no girl's love and no secret sympathy.
2. Second wish: that girls, mothers, the dying, the young, husbands and the aged all believe he can make the world happy.
3. He offers the second in case that proud soul scorns such pity and spurns the relief.

The first is that no one understand him but everyone envy him, that no friend attach himself to him, no girl love him, no secret sympathy suspect his solitary pain, no eye fathom his distant sorrow and no ear detect his secret sigh. He offers the second in case that proud soul scorns such pity and spurns the relief: that the girls love him, that women with child in their anguish seek him out, that mothers put their hopes in him, that the dying look to him for comfort, that the young attach themselves to him, that husbands depend on him and the aged reach for him as for a staff — that the whole world believe he is able to make it happy.

### Q: How does A reverse the title at the end, and what four clauses follow the reversal?
**Topic:** The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 150a60ee
**A:** Parts:
1. He corrects himself: he ought to say the happiest.
2. What is life but madness.
3. What is faith but folly.
4. What is hope but a reprieve.
5. What is love but salt in the wound.

He says farewell to the unhappiest one and then corrects himself: he ought to say the happiest, because this is a gift of fortune that nobody can give himself. Language fails and thought is confounded, for who is the happiest except the unhappiest and who the unhappiest except the happiest. Then the four clauses: what is life but madness, faith but folly, hope but a reprieve, and love but salt in the wound.

### Q: How does "The Unhappiest One" actually end, and what follows about whether the contest is settled?
**Topic:** The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** derive  **Id:** c683b4fb
**A:** Parts:
1. The winner vanishes and the fellowship stands at the empty grave again.
2. A wishes him peace, rest, an early death and eternal oblivion, so that not even his memory makes anyone unhappy.
3. A dismisses the meeting as the day begins its untiring activity again, repeating itself for ever.
4. Nothing is settled: no one is installed in the grave, which is as empty at the end as at the beginning.

The winner vanishes and the fellowship is standing at the empty grave again. A wishes him peace, rest, recovery, every possible good fortune, an early death, an eternal oblivion and no remembrance, so that not even the memory of him should make anyone else unhappy. Then he dismisses the meeting: the night has passed and the day begins its untiring activity again, never weary of repeating itself for ever, which echoes Ecclesiastes 1. Nothing is settled. No one is installed in the grave, A said at the start that the title belongs to a class rather than a person, and he told the winner that the time was perhaps not yet come and the way perhaps long. The grave is as empty at the end as at the beginning.

### Q: A says the grave itself is not the strangest thing about the opening story. What does he find stranger, and what does he say about titles?
**Topic:** The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 0850c539
**A:** Parts:
1. He finds it stranger that the grave was opened at all, that anyone took the trouble to check.
2. The inscription works like a book title so evocative and personally appealing that you will never read the book.

He asks which is the more surprising, that no corpse was found or that the grave was opened at all, and says it is strange that anyone took the trouble to check whether someone was in it. On titles: reading a name on an epitaph makes you want to climb down and talk to the man, but this inscription says so much on its own that it works the way a book title can, so evocative and so personally appealing that you will never read the book.

## Discussion of "The Unhappiest One" (2026-09-16)

### Q: Niobe lost twelve children at a single blow. The second young girl lost one lover who may not even have wronged her. Why does the second girl rank higher in A's contest?
**Topic:** The gallery in The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 3ef0df6d
**A:** Key:
1. The contest is not about how much was lost but whether either direction in time still holds a present.
2. Niobe's loss is real and complete, so she can grieve and is present in her memory.
3. The second girl can neither grieve, since perhaps he was not a deceiver, nor hope, since he was a riddle.

Because the contest is not about how much was lost but about whether either direction in time still holds a present. Niobe's loss is real and complete, so she can grieve, and grieving means she is present in her memory; A says she was unhappy for one instant and in that same instant became happy, and nothing can take that from her. The second girl cannot grieve, because perhaps he was not a deceiver, and cannot hope, because he was a riddle. She does not even know what she lost, so she has no ground behind her and none ahead. Less happened to her and she stands closer to the grave. Grief is proof of something real, and that disqualifies you.

### Q: Give A's example of a hoping individual who is not unhappy in the strict sense, and say why he is not.
**Topic:** The unhappy consciousness and the three formations  **Lec:** 3  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 79f1b34a
**A:** Key:
1. The example is the person who hopes for an eternal life.
2. He is not strictly unhappy, because he is present to himself in that hope and never conflicts with finite moments.

The person who hopes for an eternal life. A grants that he is unhappy in a certain sense, because he renounces the present, but he is not strictly unhappy, because he is present to himself in that hope and never comes into conflict with the particular moments of finite existence. Hope is not the problem; only hope you cannot be present in, for something you yourself know cannot be realised, makes the strict case.

### Q: "Planning your future carefully is a form of A's unhappy hoping." Assess.
**Topic:** The unhappy consciousness and the three formations  **Lec:** 3  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 6712f153
**A:** Key:
1. No: careful planning is not A's unhappy hoping.
2. Planning thinks about the future while staying present, and a hoper present in his hope is not strictly unhappy.
3. A's case is different in kind: the man has experienced the future in thought, so he remembers it instead of hoping for it.
4. The distinction is between working out what will happen and pre-living how it will feel.

No. Planning is thinking about the future while staying present: logistics, timing, contingencies. A planner still expects to be surprised, and A says a hoper who is present in his hope is not strictly unhappy. A's second half of the combination is different in kind: the man has taken the future up in thought, experienced it in thought, and so remembers it instead of hoping for it. The future arrives already used up. The distinction is between working out what will happen and pre-living how it will feel. A describes the second and offers no advice about either.

### Q: "A's definition of unhappiness is a stipulation, not an argument." Assess, and say what insight survives if the objection is granted.
**Topic:** The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** critique  **Id:** b1f4f51c
**A:** Key:
1. The objection holds: A never defends his definition of unhappiness as absence from oneself.
2. Define unhappiness as suffering instead and Job or Niobe wins the grave, and the essay collapses.
3. What survives: pain you can name and place is bearable in a way that pain you cannot is not.
4. So an ambiguous loss outranks a clear one.

The objection holds. A defines the unhappy one as the one who is absent from himself, borrows the past-or-future boundary from Hegel, and never defends the definition; he thanks Hegel and moves on. Define unhappiness as suffering instead and Job or Niobe wins the grave, and the essay collapses. What survives is a psychological claim that does not depend on the definition: pain you can name and place is bearable in a way that pain you cannot is not. The first girl knows he cheated and can grieve; the second cannot close the file because she does not know what happened. An ambiguous loss outranks a clear one, which is A's point without the theatre.

### Q: "A is a hedonist." Say what is right and what is wrong in that, and name the reading where the hedonist side shows most.
**Topic:** The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** critique  **Id:** edd04906
**A:** Key:
1. Wrong as a label: the course term is aesthete, which is wider than hedonist.
2. A hedonist wants pleasure; an aesthete wants experience, the interesting, the intense, the mood, and misery counts.
3. Hedonism cannot explain the Symparanekromenoi, who cultivate unhappiness as something to savour.
4. What is right: A has a hedonist mood, which shows most in "Crop Rotation".

The course term is aesthete, and it is wider than hedonist. A hedonist wants pleasure; an aesthete wants experience — the interesting, the intense, the mood — and misery counts. A hedonist would not found a club to celebrate sorrow on Fridays, and A's Symparanekromenoi cultivate unhappiness as something to savour, so "hedonist" cannot explain this essay at all. What is right in it: A does have a hedonist mood, and it shows most in "Crop Rotation", where boredom is the root of all evil and pleasures are rotated to stay ahead of it. Hedonism is one of A's moods, not his definition.

### Q: A measures a life by presence rather than by happiness. Explain the difference, say why it makes this an existentialist text, and say what answer Judge William gives that A does not.
**Topic:** The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** derive  **Id:** 8d5f2028
**A:** Parts:
1. The ordinary measure asks whether a life feels good; A asks whether the person is present to his own existence at all.
2. That shift from "are you happy" to "are you actually here" is the criterion existentialism runs on.
3. Judge William answers that you become present to yourself by choosing and committing, rather than by consuming experiences.

The ordinary measure asks whether a life feels good. A asks whether the person is present to his own existence at all: the unhappiest one may not be in the most pain, but he is nowhere, absent in both directions. That shift, from "are you happy" to "are you actually here", is the criterion existentialism will run on, which is why the essay is in this course. A diagnoses and walks off; he gives no cure. Judge William, in volume 2, takes the same criterion and answers it: you become present to yourself by choosing, by committing to something, rather than by consuming experiences.

### Q: "The happiest life is the one with the most experiences, kept fresh." Which life-view in Either/Or is that, where does the book first show it failing, and what is the Judge's alternative?
**Topic:** Why Kierkegaard uses pseudonyms  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** critique  **Id:** e281e0d0
**A:** Parts:
1. It is the aesthetic life-view, A's own.
2. The book first shows it failing in A's "Crop Rotation": novelty wears out and pleasures must be rotated ever faster.
3. The Judge's alternative is that presence comes from commitment, which needs no novelty.

It is the aesthetic life-view, A's own, stated plainly. The book first shows it failing in A's own "Crop Rotation": novelty wears out, every experience turns boring, and the pleasures have to be rotated faster and faster to stay ahead of it. The Symparanekromenoi of "The Unhappiest One" are the end state, people who have experienced everything and count themselves already dead. The Judge's diagnosis is that such a life has no self, only a sequence of moods, and depends on the outside world to supply the next one. His alternative is that presence comes from commitment, which needs no novelty; the married man, he argues, has first love continuously rather than once.

### Q: Why does the grave in "The Unhappiest One" have to stay empty for the essay to work?
**Topic:** The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** derive  **Id:** f2c95ee6
**A:** Key:
1. Every unhappy person who comes feels the grave was dug for him, which works only if nobody is in it.
2. An empty grave is a seat anyone can take, matching A's rule that the title belongs to a class.
3. The fellowship's whole activity is the contest; if someone were buried, the contest would be over.

Two reasons, both in the text. First, A says every unhappy person who comes to the grave feels it was dug for him, and that only works if nobody is in it; it is a seat anyone can take, which matches A's rule that the title belongs to a class rather than an individual. Second, the fellowship's whole activity is the contest; if someone were buried the contest would be over. So A crowns a nameless winner, tells him the time has perhaps not yet come, calls him the happiest, lets him vanish, and ends with the day beginning again. The reversal is the club's joke about itself: being the unhappiest is the one prize fortune can never take away.

## Lec 4 — Kierkegaard's life, and Kraal's reading of "The Unhappiest One" (Fri Sep 18, logged 2026-09-19 from a friend's notes)

### Q: Who was Regine Olsen, and what are the dates of the engagement, the break, and what she did afterwards?
**Topic:** Kierkegaard's family losses  **Lec:** 4  **Type:** recall  **Id:** a5f8a378
**A:** Parts:
1. Regine Olsen was the woman Kierkegaard was engaged to.
2. He proposed on 8 September 1840 and she accepted on 10 September.
3. He returned the ring on 11 August 1841, and the break was made final in person in October 1841.
4. She became engaged to Johan Frederik Schlegel in 1843 and married him on 3 November 1847.
5. They spoke again only on 17 March 1855, the day she sailed, when she wished him well.

The woman Kierkegaard was engaged to, born 1822; they met in May 1837, when she was 15 and he 24. He proposed on 8 September 1840 and she accepted on 10 September. He returned the ring with a farewell letter on 11 August 1841, she refused to accept the break, and it was made final in person in October 1841, two weeks before he left for Berlin on 25 October to hear Schelling. She became engaged to Johan Frederik Schlegel, her former tutor, in 1843 and married him on 3 November 1847; he became governor of the Danish West Indies in 1855. They never spoke again after 1841 except on 17 March 1855, the day she sailed, when she stopped him in the street and wished him well; he died eight months later. His will left everything to her, because for him an engagement was as binding as a marriage; she declined it.

### Q: "Kierkegaard broke off the engagement because he did not want Regine to suffer from his depression." Is that the whole story?
**Topic:** Kierkegaard's family losses  **Lec:** 4  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 25d1f504
**A:** Key:
1. No: his depression (melancholy) is one of his own stated reasons, but not the whole story.
2. Another reason is a secret, his "thorn in the flesh", that he would have to confide to a wife and could not.
3. Another is his religious task as an author, which Regine herself later named as the reason.
4. He deliberately played the cad so that she would break with him.

It is one of his own stated reasons and the one given in lecture, so it is safe as far as it goes. His journals say there was something spectral about him that no one could endure who had to see him every day, and the ring letter says he could not make a girl happy. But he gives two more reasons. One is a secret he felt he would have to confide to a wife and could not, his "thorn in the flesh", which he repeated to Boesen on his deathbed. The other is his religious task as an author, which Regine herself later named as the reason. He also deliberately played the cad so that she would break with him. "His melancholy" is correct; "only his melancholy" is not. And it was an engagement, never a marriage.

### Q: A set of lecture notes says Kierkegaard "had a brain hemorrhage as a child". Assess.
**Topic:** Kierkegaard's family losses  **Lec:** 4  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 1b78ea66
**A:** Key:
1. No source records a childhood brain hemorrhage for Kierkegaard.
2. His brother Søren Michael died at 12 in September 1819 after a schoolyard collision, usually described as a head injury.
3. A brain hemorrhage was one cause later proposed, and rejected, for Kierkegaard's own fatal illness in 1855.
4. The childhood story that does attach to him is a fall from a tree, which he blamed for his crooked back.

No source records a childhood brain hemorrhage for Kierkegaard. The line runs two other facts together. His brother Søren Michael died at 12 in September 1819, when Søren was 6, after a collision in the schoolyard, and the injury is usually described as to the head. And a brain hemorrhage was one of the causes later proposed for Kierkegaard's own fatal illness in 1855, and rejected. The childhood story that does attach to him is a fall from a tree, which he himself blamed for his crooked back and which the medical writers doubt. What is documented is that he collapsed in the street in late September 1855 with his legs paralysed, was admitted to Frederiks Hospital on 2 October, and died there on 11 November 1855, aged 42, cause still disputed.

### Q: Name the first-authorship books in which the broken engagement is standardly read, and say in one clause each how it appears.
**Topic:** First authorship: the 8 pseudonymous works  **Lec:** 4  **Type:** recall  **Id:** f1fff0db
**A:** Parts:
1. *Either/Or*: "The Seducer's Diary" was read as his treatment of Regine, and the Judge's essays defend the life he could not enter.
2. *Repetition*: the young man finds he is recollecting the girl instead of loving her and breaks with her.
3. *Fear and Trembling*: Abraham's willingness to sacrifice Isaac is read as the sacrifice of Regine.
4. *Stages on Life's Way*: "Guilty?/Not Guilty?" quotes the August 1841 farewell letter and has Quidam break his engagement.

*Either/Or* (1843): "The Seducer's Diary" was read in Copenhagen as his own treatment of Regine and was meant to make him look like a scoundrel, while the Judge's marriage essays defend the life he could not enter. *Repetition* (October 1843): the young man finds he is already recollecting the girl instead of loving her and breaks with her; Kierkegaard rewrote the ending after learning in July 1843 that Regine was engaged. *Fear and Trembling* (same day): Abraham's willingness to sacrifice Isaac, and his faith in getting him back, is read as the sacrifice of Regine. *Stages on Life's Way* (1845): "Guilty?/Not Guilty?" quotes the August 1841 farewell letter almost word for word and has Quidam break his engagement because marriage would block the religious life.

### Q: Kraal compared the end of "The Unhappiest One" with Tolstoy's *On Life*. State the parallel and the two differences, and say whether there is any historical connection.
**Topic:** Kraal on The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** 4  **Type:** derive  **Id:** 1860a025
**A:** Parts:
1. The parallel: a person whose life is located outside the present does not really live and so cannot really die.
2. First difference: Tolstoy has a positive doctrine, subject the animal self to reason and you live now, while A offers no cure.
3. Second difference: Tolstoy's target is other-worldly religion, not memory and hope.
4. There is no historical connection: Tolstoy wrote thirty years after Kierkegaard died, and the comparison is Kraal's.

The parallel: both say that a person whose life is located outside the present does not really live and therefore cannot really die. Tolstoy's *On Life* (1886–87) argues that true life exists only in the present, outside time, and that the fear of death is an illusion produced by mistaking one's temporal, animal existence for life; his sharpest line is that the thought of a future death is only the transfer into the future of a death already happening in the present. That is the twin of A's man who in a way cannot die because he has not lived. Two differences: Tolstoy has a positive doctrine, subject the animal self to reason and you live now, whereas A offers no cure and walks off; and Tolstoy's target is other-worldly religion that puts the good of life before birth or after death, not memory and hope. No connection: Tolstoy wrote thirty years after Kierkegaard died, there is no evidence he read this essay, and the comparison is Kraal's, not the literature's.

### Q: What did Kraal call the ending of "The Unhappiest One", what does the word mean, and what in the text does it name?
**Topic:** Kraal on The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** 4  **Type:** recall  **Id:** e47c5c62
**A:** Parts:
1. Kraal called it a doxology.
2. A doxology is a short hymn of praise, the "Glory be to the Father" kind.
3. It names the close, where A hails the winner with honorific titles, pronounces two sets of wishes and reverses the title to "the happiest".

A doxology: a short hymn of praise, the "Glory be to the Father" kind. It is Kraal's own label rather than a term from the literature, and it names the close of the address, where A hails the winner with a chain of honorific titles — emissary from the kingdom of sighs, elected favourite of suffering, apostle of sorrow, silent friend of pain, unhappy lover of memory — salutes him as the great unknown whose name he does not know, pronounces two opposite sets of wishes over him, and then reverses the title to "the happiest".

### Q: Kraal said the ending offers "not joy but comfort", and spoke of "the paradox of being victimised by the world". Put both in A's own words, and say which of Kraal's words are not in the text.
**Topic:** Kraal on The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** 4  **Type:** critique  **Id:** fb4aff33
**A:** Parts:
1. A's word is not comfort but fortune, *Lykke*: being the unhappiest is "a gift of fortune that no one can give himself".
2. The paradox is that the worst misfortune is a distinction the world cannot take away, so A corrects himself to "the happiest".
3. For "victimised", A says the whole world stands over against him as a single "Thou", one person, misunderstanding.
4. Neither "comfort" nor "victimised" is A's word.

A's word is not comfort but fortune, *Lykke*. Being the unhappiest is "a gift of fortune that no one can give himself", the fellowship promises to gather to envy his fortune, and earlier the reward for being sure of the title is that one need never fear good fortune. That is the paradox: the worst misfortune is a distinction the world cannot take away, which is why A corrects himself to "the happiest" and says language fails. "Victimised" is also not A's word. He says the unhappiest one has the whole world over against him as a single "Thou" with which he is in conflict, that the whole rest of the world is for him one person, misunderstanding, and that his lips smile at the world that misunderstands him. Kraal's "comfort" is a fair reading of what the four closing clauses do, since faith, hope and love are each cut down to something an aesthete can bear; on an MC option the words are the happiest, a gift of fortune, and envy.

### Q: "You can only be happy in the present." Where does A say this, and what is its status in the argument?
**Topic:** Kraal on The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** 4  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 24b7fa81
**A:** Parts:
1. He slips it in as a conditional while introducing the hoping and remembering individuals.
2. The wording is "if otherwise it is only the person who is present to himself that is happy".
3. He never argues for it, yet it is the premise the whole contest runs on.

He slips it in as a conditional while introducing the hoping and remembering individuals: they are unhappy in a sense "if otherwise it is only the person who is present to himself that is happy". He never argues for it. It is the premise the whole contest runs on: on A's view a person is happy only insofar as he is present to himself, so absence in past or future is what unhappiness is. That is why the objection that his definition is a stipulation bites exactly here.

### Q: Lecture notes say the unhappiest one is "unable to die, live, hate, or be strong". Give A's actual wording for each, and the pattern they share.
**Topic:** Kraal on The Unhappiest One  **Lec:** 4  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 515acb02
**A:** Parts:
1. Die is right: in a way he cannot die, for he has never lived.
2. Live is right: in a way he cannot live, for he is already dead.
3. A does not say he cannot hate: he hates the world only because he loves it.
4. On strength, he is powerless not for lack of strength but because his own strength makes him impotent.
5. The pattern is that each capacity is cancelled by its own opposite.

Die and live are right: in a way he cannot die, for he has never lived; in a way he cannot live, for he is already dead. A does not say he cannot hate; he says he hates the world only because he loves it, and that he has no passion not because he lacks it but because in the same instant he has the opposite one. On strength, he is powerless not because he lacks strength but because his own strength makes him impotent. The pattern is that each capacity is cancelled by its own opposite, so he is never on one side of anything, which is the same symmetry as never young and never old.

## Reading: *Either/Or*, "Crop Rotation" (Sep 21–23; logged 2026-09-21)

### Q: Give the full title of "Crop Rotation" as printed, and say what the epigraph is and what it says.
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 903c9fd1
**A:** Parts:
1. The title is "Crop Rotation: An Attempt at a Theory of Social Prudence".
2. The epigraph is from Aristophanes' *Plutus*, lines 189 and following.
3. Chremylos says one gets sick of everything, love, music, honour and the rest, and the slave Karion caps each with a food.

"Crop Rotation: An Attempt at a Theory of Social Prudence" (Danish *Vexel-Driften. Forsøg til en social Klogskabslære*; Hong has "Rotation of Crops: A Venture in a Theory of Social Prudence"). The epigraph is from Aristophanes' *Plutus*, lines 189 and following: Chremylos says one finally gets sick of everything — of love, music, honour, courage, ambition, command — and the slave Karion caps each with a food, bread, sweets, cakes, figs, barley, lentils. Satiety with everything is the essay's starting note.

### Q: What principle does A open "Crop Rotation" with, and what does he say about the force such a principle has?
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 2053902e
**A:** Parts:
1. He opens with the principle that all people are boring.
2. The principle has the repelling force one always demands of "the negative", the real principle of motion.
3. It is infinitely deterrent, so whoever has it behind him has infinite momentum for making discoveries.

That all people are boring. He says experienced people hold that starting from a principle is very sensible, so he obliges them. The principle has the repelling force one always demands of "the negative", which is the real principle of motion; it is not merely repelling but infinitely deterrent, so whoever has it behind him has infinite momentum for making discoveries. Then the line the essay is known for: boredom is a root of all evil. The Danish says *a* root (*en Rod*), which is worth knowing because the course wording has "the root".

### Q: "Idleness is the root of all evil, so work is the cure." What does A do with that proverb, and what is the difference between idleness and boredom on his account?
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 7dfdfc98
**A:** Key:
1. He calls the proverb plebeian and replaces it: boredom, not idleness, is the root of evil.
2. Idleness as such is a truly divine life, as long as one is not bored, as with the Olympian gods.
3. Boredom is a different thing altogether and can afflict the busiest people.
4. People oppose idleness to work, assuming man's destiny is to work; A opposes boredom to amusement, assuming it is to amuse himself.

He calls it plebeian and replaces it. Idleness as such is not a root of evil; it is a truly divine life, as long as one is not bored. The Olympian gods were not bored and lived happily in happy idleness, and a beautiful woman who neither sews nor spins nor irons nor reads nor plays music is happy in idleness because she is not bored. Idleness is even a mark of the human: whoever has no sense for it has not risen above the animals, who must always be in motion. Boredom is the real root of evil, the thing that has to be kept away, and it is a different thing altogether: it can afflict the busiest people. The Latin proverb that idleness is the devil's pillow is right, A says, but the devil gets no time to lay his head on the pillow if one is not bored. People assume that man's destiny is to work, so their opposition is idleness against work; A assumes man's destiny is to amuse himself, so his opposition is boredom against amusement.

### Q: Why, on A's account, can work not cure boredom?
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** derive  **Id:** 0082b72d
**A:** Key:
1. Work is the opposite of idleness, not of boredom.
2. Boredom is annulled only by amusement.
3. The busiest workers are the most boring of all, and when not bored they have simply not noticed it.

Because work is the opposite of idleness, not of boredom. Work can annul idleness, since idleness is its contrary, but boredom is annulled only by amusement. The evidence A gives is that the busiest workers, the insects whirring most industriously, are the most boring people of all, and when they are not bored it is only because they have no idea what boredom is, which means boredom has not been annulled but merely not noticed. Boredom is partly an immediate genius and partly an acquired immediacy, and the English are its paradigm nation: the travelling Englishman, a heavy immovable marmot whose whole vocabulary is one interjection.

### Q: What does A say about children and nursemaids, and what point is it making?
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 2d792dfa
**A:** Parts:
1. As long as children are amused they are good; they turn unruly when they start to be bored.
2. One hires a nursemaid partly on whether she can amuse the children, and would dismiss her for lacking it.
3. The point is that the nursemaid is the only relationship in which aesthetics gets its due.
4. Nobody divorces a wife or deposes a king for being boring; habit and boredom have taken over, so the world goes backwards.

Everyone recognises how ruinous boredom is where children are concerned. As long as children are amused they are good, and when they turn unruly at play it is because they are starting to be bored. So when hiring a nursemaid one looks not only for sobriety, faithfulness and decency but also, aesthetically, at whether she can amuse the children, and would dismiss her for lacking that even if she had every other virtue. The point is that the nursemaid is the only relationship in which aesthetics gets its due: nobody could get a divorce because a wife was boring, a king deposed for being boring to look at, a priest exiled for being boring to hear, a minister dismissed or a journalist executed for being boring. Habit and boredom have taken over, and that is why the world goes backwards.

### Q: Give A's history of the world as a history of boredom, through to Rome and the present day.
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 25af7830
**A:** Parts:
1. The gods were bored, so they created human beings.
2. Adam was bored alone, so Eve was created, and boredom grew exactly as the population grew.
3. To amuse themselves people built the tower of Babel, were dispersed over the earth, and went on being bored.
4. What held off Rome's fall was bread and circuses.
5. Today people hasten the fall by economies, so A proposes Denmark borrow millions and spend it on public amusement.

The gods were bored, so they created human beings. Adam was bored alone, so Eve was created, and from that moment boredom entered the world and grew exactly as the population grew: Adam bored alone, then Adam and Eve bored together, then Adam and Eve and Cain and Abel bored as a family, then the peoples bored en masse. To amuse themselves they built a tower reaching into the clouds, an idea as boring as the tower was tall; then they were dispersed over the earth, as people now travel abroad, and went on being bored. Man stood high and fell far, first through Eve, then from the tower of Babel. What held off Rome's fall was bread and circuses. Today, instead of diversions, people hasten the fall: convening an assembly of the estates, paying off the national debt by economies. A's counter-proposal is that Denmark borrow millions and spend it on public amusement, so that everything is free, Copenhagen becomes another Athens, and the king is whoever tells the best joke.

### Q: A divides the boring into two classes. Name them, say which is which, and give the line about dying of boredom.
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** e44c7733
**A:** Parts:
1. Those who bore others are the plebs, the crowd, the endless rabble of humanity.
2. Those who bore themselves are the elect, the nobility.
3. At boredom's peak the elect either die of boredom, the passive form, or shoot themselves out of curiosity, the active form.

The word "boring" can mean someone who bores others or someone who bores himself. Those who bore others are the plebs, the crowd, the endless rabble of humanity; those who bore themselves are the elect, the nobility. The odd thing is that those who do not bore themselves generally bore others, while those who bore themselves entertain others. The people who are never bored are the busy ones, and they are the most boring and intolerable of all. The noble class bore themselves so thoroughly that they provide diversion for the initiated even when boredom reaches its peak, at which point they either, in the passive form, die of boredom, or, in the active form, shoot themselves out of curiosity. The line is about the elect at their extreme, not a general thesis.

### Q: What is "boredom is the demonic pantheism"? Explain the phrase and the image A attaches to it.
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** derive  **Id:** 38e8c7e0
**A:** Key:
1. Pantheism ordinarily means fullness; boredom is the reverse, built on emptiness, and so a pantheistic category: everywhere, as nothing.
2. It rests on the nothing that winds through existence, and its dizziness is that of looking down into an infinite abyss.

Pantheism ordinarily means fullness, everything filled with the divine. Boredom is the reverse, built on emptiness, and is for that very reason a pantheistic category: it is everywhere, in everything, as nothing. It rests on the nothing that winds through existence, and its dizziness is the dizziness of looking down into an infinite abyss. If one remains in it as such it becomes the evil; as soon as it is annulled it is true, and it is annulled only by amusing oneself, ergo one ought to amuse oneself. A also says an eccentric diversion carries boredom inside it, which is why the diversion echoes without resonance: there is not even enough in nothing for an echo.

### Q: A distinguishes two kinds of crop rotation. Give both, say which he rejects and with what Hegelian term, and give the Nero and Marcus Aurelius contrast.
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 40b21ed9
**A:** Parts:
1. The vulgar, inartistic method is changing the soil: moving to the capital, going abroad, to America, eating off silver then gold.
2. A's own method is the true crop rotation: keep the soil and change the method of cultivation and the kind of crop.
3. A rejects the first as "the bad infinity", Hegel's term for an endless series that never arrives.
4. Nero burned half of Rome to see Troy burn; Marcus Aurelius was wiser: it is in your power to live again.

Everyone who is bored cries for change, and A agrees, but on principle. The vulgar, inartistic method is changing the soil: tired of the country you move to the capital, tired of your country you go abroad, "tired of Europe" you go to America, then you dream of travelling from star to star; or tired of eating off porcelain you eat off silver, then gold, then you burn half of Rome to see Troy burn. A calls this "the bad infinity", Hegel's term for an endless series that never arrives, and says the method cancels itself. What did Nero achieve? The emperor Antoninus, Marcus Aurelius, was wiser: it is in your power to live again, see things again as you once saw them, that is what living again is. A's own method is the true crop rotation, keeping the soil and changing the method of cultivation and the kind of crop.

### Q: State A's principle of limitation, the examples he gives for it, and the pair of words that names the contrast.
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 4e75967c
**A:** Parts:
1. The principle of limitation: the more one limits oneself, the more inventive one becomes.
2. A solitary prisoner for life is extremely inventive, and a spider can be great entertainment to him.
3. At school one keeps a fly under a nutshell, or finds the monotonous dripping from the roof entertaining.
4. The contrast is extensive against intensive: relief is sought not through extensity but through intensity.

The principle of limitation is "the only saving principle in the world": the more one limits oneself, the more inventive one becomes. A solitary prisoner for life is extremely inventive, and a spider can be great entertainment to him. Schooldays, when teachers are chosen without aesthetic regard and are therefore boring: catching a fly and keeping it under a nutshell and watching it run about with it; cutting a hole in the desk, shutting a fly in and looking down at it through paper; how entertaining the monotonous dripping from the roof can be; what a thorough observer one becomes. The contrast is extensive against intensive: relief is sought not through extensity but through intensity.

### Q: In the art of remembering and forgetting in "Crop Rotation", what does A say about hope, about *nil admirari*, and about remembering poetically?
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 74f0fc6d
**A:** Parts:
1. First throw hope overboard, since as long as one hopes one cannot limit oneself.
2. *Nil admirari*: no moment should mean so much that one cannot forget it at will, yet enough to recall it at will.
3. To remember poetically is really to forget, because the experience has already shed everything painful.

Every change of method falls under the general rule of the relation between remembering and forgetting, the two currents in which all of life moves. First throw hope overboard: only then does one begin to live artistically, since as long as one hopes one cannot limit oneself. Hope was one of Prometheus's dubious gifts; instead of the immortals' foreknowledge he gave men hope. Forgetting is an art that must be practised in advance; how one forgets depends on how one remembers, and that on how one experiences. So *nil admirari*, wonder at nothing, is the real wisdom of life: no moment should mean so much that one cannot forget it at will, yet each should mean enough that one can recall it at will. Childhood remembers best and forgets easiest. To remember poetically is really to forget, because the experience has already shed everything painful. Never enjoy to the last drop, since then one can neither remember nor forget but is left with a surfeit; when enjoyment carries you away, stop for a moment and remember, and never set all sails. Forgetting is not forgetfulness, and it must apply to the pleasant too, since even the pleasant as past carries the unpleasantness of longing. Forgetting is the scissors with which one cuts away what one cannot use, under the highest supervision of memory; the two are identical, and that artificially produced identity is the Archimedean point from which one lifts the world. Then one can play shuttlecock with the whole of existence, and a person's elasticity is measured by his power to forget.

### Q: In the art of remembering and forgetting in "Crop Rotation", what does A say about enjoyment, about forgetting as scissors, and about playing shuttlecock?
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 167f34ca
**A:** Parts:
1. Never enjoy to the last drop, or one can neither remember nor forget and is left with a surfeit.
2. Forgetting is the scissors that cut away what one cannot use.
3. With that art one can play shuttlecock with the whole of existence.

These three come after hope, *nil admirari* and poetic remembering. When enjoyment carries you away, stop for a moment and remember, and never set all sails. The scissors work under the highest supervision of memory, and a person's elasticity is measured by his power to forget.

### Q: Name the three relationships A says to avoid, and give his reason for each.
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 5ecd5ea4
**A:** Parts:
1. Friendship: a friend is not the necessary other but the superfluous third.
2. Marriage: love promised for ever means nothing, whereas "till Easter" would mean something, and divorce is hard.
3. An official post makes one a tiny peg in the machine of the state, with a title one never gets rid of.

Friendship, marriage and an official post, because each makes one "several" and costs one's freedom. Friendship: a friend is not what philosophy calls the necessary other but the superfluous third; the ancient mark of friendship, wanting and not wanting the same things, is also extremely boring; one person can be nothing for another except in the way. Marriage: lovers promise love for ever, which is easy and means nothing, whereas "till Easter" would mean something one might keep; after a while each side discovers it is wrong and their mutual faithlessness cancels to mutual contentment, but divorce is hard, so marriage must be propped up with moral supports; and with a wife it is difficult to order travelling boots, with wife and children impossible. An official post: one becomes a tiny peg in the machine of the state, ceases to be the master of one's own cultivation, and gets a title, which one never gets rid of again. None of this means inactivity or a life without contact or without the erotic; it means always having enough speed to run from a relationship, and cultivating breadless arts intensively.

### Q: "A thinks marriage is the worst thing you can do, and if you truly loved someone you would break it off." Assess against the text.
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** critique  **Id:** fd05c8aa
**A:** Key:
1. Half right: A does say never enter marriage.
2. He does say that two people destined for each other should have the courage to break off, since there is everything to lose.
3. He does not say "if you truly loved them": his ground is aesthetic self-preservation, not the beloved's good.

Half right. A does say never enter marriage, that woman is and remains man's ruin as soon as one contracts a lasting relationship with her, that a woman's love is only pretence and weakness, and that a young man fiery as an Arab horse is lost the moment he marries. And he does say that when two people fall in love and sense they are destined for each other, the thing is to have the courage to break off, since by continuing there is everything to lose and nothing to gain, which is a paradox for feeling but not for the understanding. What he does not say is "if you truly loved them". His ground is not love but the erotic's proper form: it should have infinity, but poetic infinity, which can be bounded in an hour as easily as in a month, and it requires using moods to produce inexhaustible combinations. The reason is aesthetic self-preservation, not the beloved's good. And all of it is A's, the aesthete's, position; Judge William's essay on the aesthetic validity of marriage in volume 2 is the direct reply.

### Q: "The whole secret lies in arbitrariness." Explain, with A's examples.
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 5fdbdc74
**A:** Key:
1. Arbitrariness is the inner counterpart of the accidental outside: one enjoys something one arbitrarily puts into a thing, not the thing itself.
2. Examples are watching the middle of a play and reading the third part of a book.
3. A watched a chattering philosopher's sweat drops gather and hang at his nose tip, and even spurred him to lecture.
4. Baggesen objected to a decent man that nothing rhymes with his name.
5. One constantly changes the eye with which one sees reality and keeps an open eye for the accidental.

Arbitrariness is A's word for the inner counterpart of the accidental outside. One enjoys not the thing itself but something quite different that one arbitrarily puts into it: watching the middle of a play, reading the third part of a book, and so getting an enjoyment the author never intended. His example is a man whose philosophical chatter he was obliged to listen to; near despair he noticed the man sweated heavily when he talked, watched the drops gather on his forehead, run down his nose and hang at the tip, and from then on could even spur him to lecture just to watch. He cites Baggesen's objection to a decent man that nothing rhymes with his name. One makes something accidental into the absolute and an object of absolute admiration, treats everything in life as a wager, and constantly changes the eye with which one sees reality; the degree of consistency shows whether one is an artist or a bungler. So one must always keep an open eye for the accidental and be ready, since the most trivial thing can by chance become rich material, while pleasures prepared a fortnight in advance mean little.

### Q: "A's solution is to embrace boredom through routine." Assess.
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 2c4abefc
**A:** Key:
1. The claim is backwards: A neither embraces boredom nor recommends routine.
2. Boredom is the root of all evil and must be annulled, and only amusement annuls it.
3. Routine is what he flees: habit is what he blames for the world's decline.
4. He proposes new methods on the same soil: limitation, intensive rotation, remembering and forgetting, and arbitrariness.

Backwards on both halves. A does not embrace boredom: it is the root of all evil and must be annulled, and the only thing that annuls it is amusement. And routine is the thing he is fleeing. Habit is what he blames for the world's decline, and marriage is condemned partly because it lands you in a fatal continuity with custom, which is as unpredictable as wind and weather. What he proposes is change, but change of the right kind: not new soil, new places and new things, which is the bad infinity, but new methods on the same soil, the principle of limitation, intensive rather than extensive rotation, governed by the arts of remembering and forgetting and by arbitrariness. The prisoner with his spider is not embracing boredom; he is defeating it with almost nothing.

## Lec 5–6 — Crop Rotation in lecture (Mon Sep 21, Wed Sep 23; logged 2026-09-25 from a friend's notes)

### Q: Tell the plot of Aristophanes' *Plutus* and say what Kraal used it for.
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** 5–6  **Type:** recall  **Id:** c76d17c5
**A:** Parts:
1. Chremylos, a poor honest farmer, asks Delphi whether to raise his son a crook and is told to follow the first man he meets.
2. That man is a blind beggar who turns out to be Plutus, god of wealth, blinded by Zeus.
3. His sight is restored at the temple of Asclepius, wealth flows to the honest, and nobody needs the gods any more.
4. Kraal used it as the setup for "Crop Rotation": a world where everyone has what they need has nothing left to want.

Chremylos, a poor honest farmer, asks the oracle at Delphi whether his son should be raised a crook, since only crooks prosper, and is told to follow the first man he meets. It is a blind beggar who turns out to be Plutus, the god of wealth, blinded by Zeus so he cannot tell the just from the unjust. Chremylos takes him to the temple of Asclepius, his sight is restored, and wealth flows to the honest, with the comic result that nobody needs the gods any more: Hermes turns up starving and the priest of Zeus is out of work. Kraal used it as the setup for "Crop Rotation": a world where everyone has what they need has nothing left to want, which is the satiety of the epigraph and the boredom of the essay. The "society degrades for lack of motivation" ending is his gloss, not the play's.

### Q: What did Kierkegaard think of Aristophanes' portrait of Socrates, and where does he say it?
**Topic:** Kierkegaard 1841–43  **Lec:** 5–6  **Type:** recall  **Id:** f883e0ef
**A:** Parts:
1. He thought Aristophanes' comic portrait in the *Clouds* came closest to the truth.
2. He says it in his dissertation *On the Concept of Irony*.

In the 1841 dissertation *On the Concept of Irony* he weighs the three sources, Xenophon, Plato and Aristophanes' *Clouds*, and argues that the comic portrait comes closest to the truth, because Socrates was pure irony and only a comedian could catch that. Hence Kraal's remark that he thought Aristophanes' account the most reliable.

### Q: What does A say about man as a "beast of prey"? How should Kraal's line from Fourier to Mill to Nietzsche be understood, and why?
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** 5–6  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 5b38cf88
**A:** Parts:
1. A says that, for all the talk of man as a social animal, he is at bottom a beast of prey, as his teeth show.
2. The talk of society is partly inherited hypocrisy and partly calculated cunning.
3. Kraal's line from Fourier to Mill to Nietzsche is context, not influence.
4. Mill's *Utilitarianism* is 1861 and Nietzsche writes in the 1880s, so A can have read neither.

A says that for all the talk of man being a social animal, he is at bottom a beast of prey, something one can confirm by looking at his teeth, and that the whole talk of society is partly inherited hypocrisy and partly calculated cunning. He does not coin the phrase; it is an ordinary word used with a sneer. Kraal placed this in a nineteenth-century argument about altruism: Fourier's utopian schemes, Mill's utilitarianism, Nietzsche's suspicion of the heart. That is context, not influence: Fourier is earlier than 1843, but Mill's *Utilitarianism* is 1861 and Nietzsche writes in the 1880s, so A can have read neither.

### Q: Kraal presented A's cure for boredom as three arts, with Hannay page numbers. Give them.
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** 5–6  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 1a88b04b
**A:** Parts:
1. The art of self-limitation, at page 233.
2. The art of forgetting and remembering, also at page 233.
3. The art of arbitrariness, at pages 239–240.

The bad infinity, varying the soil, is at page 232. The three arts are at 233: the art of self-limitation, which produces inventiveness because having fewer options makes any one easier to enjoy; the art of forgetting and remembering, which must apply to the pleasant as well as the unpleasant, since the pleasant once past carries longing; and the art of arbitrariness, at 239–240, allowing whimsy and spontaneity rather than fixing everything by plan. The numbering into three is Kraal's; A gives the principle of limitation, then remembering and forgetting, then arbitrariness, without counting them.

### Q: How does A say boredom usually shows itself, as opposed to being reasoned to?
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** 5–6  **Type:** recall  **Id:** f4d83cde
**A:** Key:
1. Boredom usually shows itself as immediacy.
2. It is partly an immediate genius and partly an acquired immediacy.

Mostly as immediacy. A says boredom is partly an immediate genius and partly an acquired immediacy, and his picture of it is the travelling Englishman whose whole vocabulary is a single interjection, in which admiration and indifference have collapsed into one. Kraal's version was that a bare "oh" tells you at once that something is boring. Reasoning your way to boredom is the rarer case.

### Q: Kraal's word for A's rule on friendship, marriage and vocation was "an exit door". Say what A actually says about each, and what the exit protects.
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** 5–6  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 9010efc0
**A:** Parts:
1. Friendship: a friend is the superfluous third; keep more speed than the relationship so you can run from it.
2. Marriage: never enter it; love promised for ever means nothing where "till Easter" would mean something.
3. Vocation: never take an official post, or you become a peg in the machine of the state with a title you cannot shed.
4. The exit protects freedom: never becoming "several" and never being unable to leave.

What it protects is freedom, which A understands as never becoming "several" and never being unable to leave. Friendship: a friend is the superfluous third, not the necessary other; keep more speed than the relationship so you can run from it, and let it lie fallow like a field. Marriage: never enter it; love promised for ever means nothing where "till Easter" would mean something; with a wife you cannot order travelling boots, with children it is impossible; and the lines Kraal softened, that woman is man's ruin and a woman's love is pretence and weakness. Vocation: never take an official post, because you become a peg in the machine of the state and acquire a title you can never shed. "Just another lawyer" was Kraal's example.

## Lec 7 — The aesthetic recipe, and the start of "Ancient Tragedy" (Fri Sep 25, logged 2026-09-25)

### Q: Give Kraal's three-part summary of how satisfaction is best served on A's aesthetic view, with the essay attached to each part.
**Topic:** The aesthetic life-view  **Lec:** 7  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 61dbb35f
**A:** Parts:
1. First, a pessimistic baseline, finding happiness inside unhappiness, which is "The Unhappiest One".
2. Second, having no deeper lifelong commitments, which is "Crop Rotation".
3. Third, seeing one's fortune or misfortune as beyond one's control, which is "Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern".

Satisfaction is best served, first, by a pessimistic baseline, finding happiness inside unhappiness, which is "The Unhappiest One"; second, by having no deeper lifelong commitments, which is "Crop Rotation"; and third, by seeing one's fortune or misfortune as something beyond one's control, which is "Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern". The synthesis is Kraal's, not A's.

### Q: "Aesthetic" has two uses in this course. Give both, say which one dominates the tragedy essay, and give A's one-sentence map of the three spheres.
**Topic:** The aesthetic life-view  **Lec:** 7  **Type:** recall  **Id:** c4e9bc21
**A:** Parts:
1. The narrow use is the artistic category: what counts as beautiful, tragic, comic or interesting.
2. The wide use is the aesthetic life-view, the first sphere: living for immediacy, moods, enjoyment and the interesting.
3. The tragedy essay uses the narrow sense almost throughout.
4. The aesthetic gives rest before sin's opposition is asserted, the ethical is strict and hard, the religious gives rest only after its full terror.

The narrow use is the artistic category: what counts as beautiful, tragic, comic or interesting, as in "aesthetic validity", "aesthetic interest" and "aesthetic guilt". The wide use is the aesthetic life-view, the first of the three spheres: living for immediacy, moods, enjoyment and the interesting, and judging a life by what it is like to experience rather than by duty or by God. The tragedy essay uses the narrow sense almost throughout; when A says sin is not an aesthetic element he means it is not material for art. His map: the aesthetic gives rest before the deep opposition of sin has been asserted, the ethical is strict and hard, and the religious gives rest only after that opposition has been seen in its full terror; the tragic is a motherly love that soothes, the religious a fatherly one.

### Q: Kraal summed up A's rule for relationships as "there must always be an exit". A student objects that there is meaning in struggle and that leaving at the first difficulty is a slippery slope. Whose position is the objection, and how does the book handle it?
**Topic:** The aesthetic life-view  **Lec:** 7  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 0aa90d50
**A:** Key:
1. The objection is Judge William's position.
2. The Judge's "The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage" replies that commitment lets first love last and the ethical life is more beautiful.
3. The book does not settle it: Eremita's preface leaves the views facing each other.

The objection is Judge William's, almost word for word. A's freedom is the absence of ties: friendship, marriage and office are each condemned because they make one "several" and cost the ability to leave. The Judge's first letter in volume 2, "The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage", replies that commitment is what lets first love last and that the ethical life is the more beautiful one. The book does not settle it; Eremita's preface says the views are left facing each other. On an exam, attribute the exit rule to A and the meaning-in-commitment reply to B.

### Q: Kraal connected satisfaction-in-unhappiness to Camus's *The Stranger*. Say what the connection is and give two cautions.
**Topic:** The aesthetic life-view  **Lec:** 7  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 8db64519
**A:** Parts:
1. Meursault's apathetic, pessimistic indifference in *The Stranger* is what Kraal saw as a later relative of A's satisfaction inside unhappiness.
2. First caution: Camus refused both labels, nihilist and existentialist; his word was the absurd.
3. Second caution: Meursault does not cultivate his indifference as A cultivates arbitrariness, so the parallel is a mood, not a method.

Meursault, the narrator of *The Stranger* (1942), is indifferent to his mother's death, his lover and his own trial, and that apathetic, pessimistic attitude is what Kraal had in mind as a later relative of A's finding satisfaction inside unhappiness. First caution: Camus refused both labels, nihilist and existentialist; his word was the absurd, and *The Myth of Sisyphus* argues against nihilism. Second: Meursault does not cultivate his indifference the way A cultivates arbitrariness; he simply has it, so the parallel is a mood, not a method. Camus is the last lecture of the course, on December 7.

### Q: What is meant by "French existentialism" in this course, what does it take from Kierkegaard, and what does it drop?
**Topic:** The aesthetic life-view  **Lec:** 7  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 6da8b48c
**A:** Parts:
1. French existentialism means Sartre and de Beauvoir, with Camus usually filed beside them though he rejected the label.
2. From Kierkegaard they take the single individual against the system, existence as choice, anxiety, and truth as lived rather than proved.
3. They drop God, the point the whole authorship is aimed at.

Sartre and de Beauvoir, with Camus usually filed beside them though he rejected the label; they are the last week of the course, December 4 and 7. From Kierkegaard they take the single individual set against the system, existence as choice, anxiety, and truth as something lived rather than proved. What they drop is God, which is the point the whole authorship is aimed at, so the "founder of existentialism" title fits him only in hindsight.

### Q: State A's core contrast between ancient and modern tragedy, and give the sentence at Hannay page 143.
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 7  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 4dc1a4a9
**A:** Parts:
1. In ancient tragedy the individual rests in substantial ties, the state, the family and fate, so the action is "as much event as action".
2. In modern tragedy the hero is reflected out of every immediate relation to state, kin and fate.
3. The sentence at page 143 is that the hero "stands and falls entirely on his own deeds".

In ancient tragedy the individual, however freely he moves, rests in substantial ties, the state, the family and fate, so the action is only partly his and has a share of suffering in it, "as much event as action"; that is why there is a chorus, a monologue and an epic element, and the hero's downfall is a suffering as well as a deed. In modern tragedy the hero is reflected out of every immediate relation to state, kin and fate and often out of his own past; situation and character dominate, everything is exhausted in dialogue, and there is no epic foreground and no epic remainder. The sentence: the hero "stands and falls entirely on his own deeds".

### Q: Explain A's account of tragic guilt, the two extremes it lies between, and his verdict on the modern age's handling of it.
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 7  **Type:** derive  **Id:** cf8588c4
**A:** Parts:
1. Aristotle requires hamartia, and in Greek tragedy the guilt lies between doing and suffering, an ambiguity that is the tragic collision.
2. With no guilt at all, the tragic collision goes slack.
3. With absolute guilt the hero is simply bad: his aesthetic guilt has become ethical guilt, and evil has no aesthetic interest.
4. The modern age errs by loading the hero's whole life onto him as his own deed, refusing an unhappy childhood as an excuse.
5. A's verdict is that the courage to be one's own creator is an illusion, and "as the age loses the tragic, it gains despair."

Aristotle requires the hero to have hamartia. In Greek tragedy the action lies between doing and suffering, so the guilt does too, and that ambiguity is the tragic collision. The tragic lies between two extremes: with no guilt the collision goes slack, and with absolute guilt the hero stops interesting us tragically, because his aesthetic guilt has become ethical guilt, he is simply bad, and evil has no aesthetic interest; sin is not an aesthetic element. The modern age errs by loading the hero's whole life onto him as his own deed and refusing an unhappy childhood as an excuse. A's verdict: the courage to be one's own creator is an illusion, and "as the age loses the tragic, it gains despair."

### Q: Why is Sophocles' *Antigone* A's principal example of ancient tragedy, and what would turn it into a modern subject?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 7  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 8b4f2340
**A:** Parts:
1. The trilogy turns on inherited guilt, guilt that is guilt and yet not guilt, handed down the house of Labdacus through piety.
2. What makes it Greek is that Oedipus's fate echoes through it, so that "not an individual but a little world" goes under.
3. Taken alone, the burial as a collision between sisterly love and an arbitrary human ban would be an entirely modern subject.

Because the trilogy, *Oedipus at Colonus*, *Oedipus the King* and *Antigone*, turns on inherited guilt: guilt that is guilt and yet not guilt, handed down the house of Labdacus through piety, which A compares to original sin and calls a substantial category. In *Antigone* the guilt gathers on one act, burying her brother against the king's ban. Taken alone, as a collision between sisterly love and an arbitrary human ban, that would be an entirely modern subject. What makes it Greek is that Oedipus's fate echoes through it, so that "not an individual but a little world" goes under; the Greek Antigone herself lives carefree and never dwells on her father's fate.

### Q: Kraal gave Faust and Hamlet as modern tragedies. Which of the two is in A's essay, in what connection, and what Faust does A actually mention?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 7  **Type:** critique  **Id:** c521355e
**A:** Parts:
1. Hamlet is in the essay: "Hamlet is so tragic because he suspects his mother's crime."
2. The connection is anxiety: suspicion is anxiety, a reflection toward past or future, which belongs essentially to the modern tragic.
3. Goethe's Faust is Kraal's example and is treated elsewhere in the book, not in this essay.
4. The Faust in the essay is Grabbe's *Faust and Don Juan*, which A names as a work "built on evil", his instance of absolute guilt.

Hamlet is in the text: "Hamlet is so tragic because he suspects his mother's crime." Suspicion is anxiety, and A says anxiety, a reflection that looks to past or future rather than the present, belongs essentially to the modern tragic. Goethe's Faust, the scholar's pact with the devil and the seduction of Gretchen, is Kraal's example and is treated elsewhere in the book. The Faust in this essay is Grabbe's play *Faust and Don Juan*, which A names as a work "built on evil", his instance of what goes wrong when the hero's guilt is made absolute.

### Q: A says an individual who wants to be absolute becomes comic, and one who accepts being relative gets the tragic. A student reads this as "balance the objective and the subjective." Assess.
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 7  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 380ca77a
**A:** Key:
1. The student's lesson is wrong: the passage is about guilt and belonging, not about balancing objective and subjective knowledge.
2. The individual who wants to be absolute, denying he is the child of God, his age, people, family and friends, becomes comic.
3. The individual who accepts being relative gets the tragic, which A calls a mildness and a healing.
4. It is not a middle path but a preference, on aesthetic grounds, for the relative self over the modern self-creator.

The passage is right but the lesson is wrong. A's sentence is that every individual, however original, is the child of God, his age, his people, his family and his friends, and only there has its truth; if in all that relativity it wants to be the absolute it becomes ridiculous, and if it gives up the claim it has the tragic even if it were the happiest individual, indeed is only happy when it has the tragic. It is about guilt and belonging, not knowledge: the self-creator who denies he is shaped by and partly answerable through his family and time is comic, and the one who accepts it gets the tragic, which A calls a mildness and a healing. It is not a middle path but a preference for the relative self over the modern self-creator, on aesthetic grounds, and the question of which views are objective is one the essay never raises. A's comic image is a word stuck as an adverb in one case that demands to be declined through all of them.

### Q: What is A's diagnosis of his own age at the start of the tragedy essay, and what did Kraal call it?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 7  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 8488fe4a
**A:** Parts:
1. A's diagnosis is that "the whole age tends toward the comic".
2. Isolation grows, and the associations formed against it prove it rather than cure it.
3. Everyone wants to rule and nobody wants the responsibility.
4. Kraal called it the zeitgeist, the spirit of the age.

Kraal called it the zeitgeist, the spirit of the age. A's line is that "the whole age tends toward the comic": existence is undermined by the subjects' doubt, isolation grows, the mania for forming associations proves it rather than curing it since a hundred asserting themselves as a hundred is the same isolation as one asserting himself as one, everyone wants to rule and nobody wants the responsibility, and the age is more melancholy than Aristophanes' Greece and therefore more deeply in despair. The comic lies in isolation, in subjectivity as mere form asserting itself.

### Q: Give A's distinction between sorrow and pain in tragedy, with his image for it.
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 7  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 5de273fb
**A:** Key:
1. In ancient tragedy sorrow is deeper and pain less; in modern tragedy pain is greater and sorrow less.
2. Sorrow always has something substantial in it, while pain implies reflection on the suffering.
3. A's image is a child watching an adult suffer: not reflected enough to feel pain, yet its sorrow is infinitely deep.
4. An adult watching a child suffer feels more pain and less sorrow, because the idea of guilt has entered.
5. True tragic sorrow requires a moment of guilt, and true tragic pain a moment of innocence.

In ancient tragedy sorrow is deeper and pain less; in modern tragedy pain is greater and sorrow less. Sorrow always has something substantial in it, while pain implies reflection on the suffering.
