A's image is a child watching an adult suffer: not reflected enough to feel pain, yet its sorrow is infinitely deep, and that is Greek sorrow, mild and deep at once. An adult watching a child suffer feels more pain and less sorrow, because the idea of guilt has entered. The bitterest pain is repentance, which has ethical rather than aesthetic reality. True tragic sorrow requires a moment of guilt and true tragic pain a moment of innocence.

## Lec 8 — Ancient Tragedy: guilt, sorrow, fate (Mon Sep 28, logged 2026-09-28 from a friend's notes)

### Q: A dictionary gives "regret" as the synonym for sorrow. Why is that the wrong neighbour for A's word, and which side of his distinction does each belong to?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 8  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 3d25789c
**A:** Key:
1. Sorrow belongs to the ancient side and regret to the modern side, so regret sits on the opposite side of A's distinction.
2. A's sorrow is the mild, deep, unreflected grief of ancient tragedy, which goes with fate and family.
3. Regret, or repentance, is what A names the bitterest pain, with ethical rather than aesthetic reality.
4. Pain is the modern side: reflection on suffering, guilt and the self.

A's sorrow is the mild, deep, unreflected grief of ancient tragedy, which goes with fate, family and inherited guilt (true tragic sorrow needs a moment of guilt, true tragic pain a moment of innocence). Regret, or repentance, is what he names the bitterest pain, and he says it has ethical rather than aesthetic reality and does not want to be watched. Pain is the modern side, reflection on suffering, guilt and the self. So sorrow belongs to the ancient and regret to the modern, and any option that pairs sorrow with regret or with "your own fault" is wrong.

### Q: Contrast ancient and modern tragic guilt, with the terms and the page Kraal cited.
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 8  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 76f96409
**A:** Parts:
1. Ancient guilt is aesthetic: it lies between doing and suffering and is guilt and yet not guilt.
2. A calls ancient guilt inherited guilt and compares it to original sin.
3. Modern guilt is ethical: the isolated individual is his own creator, so his guilt is sin and his pain is repentance.
4. Kraal cited Hannay page 147 for modern guilt.

Ancient guilt is aesthetic: it lies between doing and suffering, is inherited through piety, and is guilt and yet not guilt; A calls it inherited guilt and compares it to original sin, and Antigone carries the curse on the house of Labdacus without having chosen it. Modern guilt is ethical, at Hannay page 147: the isolated individual is his own creator, so his guilt is sin and his pain is repentance, and with that the tragic is cancelled. The spectator's response becomes "help yourself and heaven will help you," which is the loss of compassion, and compassion is the real expression of the tragic. A's verdict is that modern tragedy is aesthetically deficient exactly to the extent that it is ethically dominated.

### Q: What is the "healing" A finds in the tragic, at Hannay page 144, and what image does he use for it?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 8  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 509efef1
**A:** Parts:
1. The healing is that guilt is shared, inherited through family and age, so the individual is not left alone as the sole author of his fate.
2. A's image is that aesthetically the tragic is to human life what divine grace and mercy are, a motherly love that soothes the troubled.

A says there is "a melancholy and a healing" in the tragic, and that aesthetically the tragic is to human life what divine grace and mercy are, a motherly love that soothes the troubled. The ethical, by contrast, is strict and hard, and the religious is a fatherly love that contains the ethical but softens it. The healing is not that someone else is to blame; it is that guilt is shared, inherited through family and age, so the individual is not left alone as the sole author of his fate. A adds that the individual is only happy when it has the tragic, and that in trying to win itself in the supernatural way our age attempts, it loses itself and becomes comic.

### Q: Kraal linked A's praise of accepting fate to Nietzsche and called A's method "genealogical". Say what Nietzsche's idea is, when it dates from, how to hold the link, and what "genealogical" means here.
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 8  **Type:** recall  **Id:** ddbb7b30
**A:** Parts:
1. Nietzsche's idea is amor fati, love of fate: to see as beautiful what is necessary in things.
2. Amor fati first appears in The Gay Science in 1882.
3. That is forty years after A's essay, so the link is a parallel, not a debt.
4. Kraal means that A traces how the concept of tragic guilt changed as the age changed, a history of a concept.

Nietzsche's amor fati, love of fate, first appears in The Gay Science in 1882: to see as beautiful what is necessary in things. That is forty years after A's essay, so it is a parallel and not a debt, and "Nietzsche says this after Kierkegaard" is correct. "Genealogical" is also Nietzsche's word, from On the Genealogy of Morals in 1887; Kraal means that A traces how the concept of tragic guilt changed as the age changed, from the substantial ties of Greece to the isolated individual, which is a history of a concept rather than a definition. Hold both as Kraal's framing, not A's vocabulary.

### Q: What does A actually say about Christ in the tragedy essay, and how does that relate to Kraal's claim that the crucifixion is the turning point from ancient to modern tragedy?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 8  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 7493c9e4
**A:** Parts:
1. A says Christ's appearance is in one sense the deepest tragedy, because he came in the fullness of time and bore the sin of the whole world.
2. A says Christ's life is never called a tragedy, because the unity of absolute innocence and absolute guilt is metaphysical, not aesthetic.
3. In Christ suffering is absolute because it is absolutely free action, and action is absolute suffering because it is absolute obedience.
4. The crucifixion as the turning point is Kraal's reading, not a sentence in the text.
5. It is defensible, since Christianity brings the sin, responsibility and reflected individuality that A says the modern age has and Greece lacked.

Two things. First, that Christ's appearance is in one sense the deepest tragedy, because he came in the fullness of time and bore the sin of the whole world, in contrast to the comedy of a contingent individual who imagines himself the world's saviour. Second, that Christ's life is never called a tragedy because it exceeds aesthetic categories: the unity of absolute innocence and absolute guilt is metaphysical, not aesthetic, and in Christ suffering is absolute because it is absolutely free action, and action is absolute suffering because it is absolute obedience. The "turning point" is Kraal's reading, not a sentence in the text; it is defensible, since Christianity brings the sin, responsibility and reflected individuality that A says the modern age has and Greece lacked, but "absolutely free" belongs to A's second point, not to a claim about the history of drama.

### Q: Kraal asked what Antigone would look like re-imagined as a modern tragedy and answered "her suffering comes from her own decisions." What does A's own modern Antigone do instead?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 8  **Type:** critique  **Id:** d7ae0b9d
**A:** Key:
1. A's modern Antigone keeps the inherited guilt and adds reflection, instead of making her suffering come from her own decisions.
2. In A's version only she knows the secret that Oedipus killed his father and married his mother, while he lives honoured and happy.
3. She does not know whether her father himself knew, which is the unrest in her sorrow and the ambiguity in her pain.
4. Her organ is anxiety rather than sorrow, and A says anxiety belongs essentially to the modern tragic.

Kraal's version is the modern tendency, and A names it: take the burial as an isolated fact, a collision between sisterly love and an arbitrary ban, and it becomes "an entirely modern tragic subject." A's own modern Antigone keeps the inherited guilt and adds reflection. Everything is as in Sophocles except that the secret of Oedipus, that he killed his father and married his mother, is known to nobody but her; Oedipus lives honoured and happy; she carries the secret alone and does not know whether her father himself knew, which is the unrest in her sorrow and the ambiguity in her pain. Her organ is anxiety rather than sorrow, and A says anxiety belongs essentially to the modern tragic because it looks to past or future, never to the present.

### Q: Describe the collision in A's modern Antigone, and the two images he attaches to it.
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 8  **Type:** recall  **Id:** d70c8c9c
**A:** Parts:
1. She falls "mortally in love", and the beloved presses her for the secret she cannot give without shaming her father.
2. The powers hold each other so evenly that action becomes impossible, and she can confess her love only in the moment she dies.
3. The first image is Epaminondas, wounded at Mantinea, who left the arrow in until the battle was won, because drawing it out was death.
4. The second image is the prophecy that Hercules would be killed not by a living man but by a dead one.
5. She falls by the dead, since her father's memory is the ground, and by the living, since her unhappy love is the occasion.

She falls in love, "mortally in love," and the beloved, who knows he is loved, presses her for the secret she cannot give without shaming her father; he meets her at Oedipus's grave and adjures her by her love for her father, working against himself without knowing it. The powers hold each other so evenly that action becomes impossible, and she can only confess her love in the moment she dies. First image: Epaminondas, wounded at Mantinea, left the arrow in the wound until he heard the battle was won, because drawing it out was death; she carries the secret the same way. Second: the prophecy to Hercules that he would be killed not by a living man but by a dead one; she falls in one sense by the dead, since the memory of her father is the ground, and in another by the living, since her unhappy love is the occasion.

### Q: Give A's names for his modern Antigone and say what each expresses.
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 8  **Type:** recall  **Id:** ff645046
**A:** Parts:
1. "Sorrow's bride", because her sorrow is her love and she has dedicated her life to it.
2. "Virgo mater", virgin mother, because she carries her secret hidden under her heart.
3. "The daughter of sorrow with pain as her dowry", because she has the substantiality to feel sorrow and the reflection to feel pain.

"Sorrow's bride", because her sorrow is her love and she has dedicated her life to it; "virgo mater", virgin mother, because she carries her secret under her heart, hidden; and "the daughter of sorrow with pain as her dowry", because she has the substantiality to feel sorrow and the reflection to feel pain, which is why A chose a woman for the experiment. Her scene is not outward but inward, "a scene of spirit," and she is, though living, in another sense already dead, which is why she suits the Symparanekromenoi.

### Q: Tell the Sophocles story in three lines and say what A takes from it and what he sets aside.
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 8  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 78d123e0
**A:** Parts:
1. Polynices dies attacking Thebes, Creon forbids his burial, and Antigone buries him and is condemned to be walled up alive.
2. A takes the point that the tragic guilt gathers on one act.
3. A takes the point that Oedipus's fate echoes through it, so that "not an individual but a little world" goes under.
4. He sets aside her higher-law argument, the unwritten laws of the gods against a king's decree, as Sophocles' emphasis, not his.

Polynices dies attacking Thebes; Creon forbids his burial; Antigone buries him and is condemned to be walled up alive, defending herself by the unwritten laws of the gods against a king's decree. A takes the point that the tragic guilt gathers on one act and that Oedipus's fate echoes through it, so that "not an individual but a little world" goes under; that echo is what makes it Greek. He sets aside the higher-law argument, which is Sophocles' emphasis, not his, and he notes that the Greek Antigone lives carefree and never dwells on her father's fate, which is exactly what his modern Antigone cannot do.

### Q: Why does A say the Jews could seem to be the tragic people, and why does he say they are not?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** 8  **Type:** derive  **Id:** 80b0e7e7
**A:** Parts:
1. They could seem tragic because Jehovah is a jealous God who visits the fathers' sins on the children to the third and fourth generation.
2. Judaism is too ethically developed for tragedy: Jehovah's curses, however terrible, are also just punishments.
3. The wrath of the Greek gods has no ethical character, only aesthetic ambiguity, and the tragic needs that ambiguity.

Because Jehovah is a jealous God who visits the fathers' sins on the children to the third and fourth generation, and the curses of the Old Testament look like tragic material. But Judaism is too ethically developed for tragedy: Jehovah's curses, however terrible, are also just punishments, whereas the wrath of the Greek gods has no ethical character, only aesthetic ambiguity. The tragic needs that ambiguity; a just punishment is ethics, not tragedy.

## Exam 1 — the real questions (Fri Oct 2, as Matt recalled them)

### Q: Exam 1, question 1: drawing on "The Unhappiest One" and "Crop Rotation", give three ways A suggests a person fails to overcome boredom.
**Topic:** Crop Rotation  **Lec:** exam1  **Type:** apply  **Id:** da53e894
**A:** Parts:
1. Chasing novelty fails: changing the soil is the extensive method, which A calls the bad infinity and which cancels itself.
2. Getting tied down by friendship, marriage or office fails, because then you can no longer rotate.
3. Losing control of memory and hope fails, instead of throwing hope overboard and remembering and forgetting at will; The Unhappiest One portrays the rememberer, the hoper, and the unhappiest one in whom both cross.

Crop Rotation supplies the failures and The Unhappiest One shows the people they produce.
- Chasing novelty. Changing the soil, by moving to the city, going abroad or eating off gold, is the extensive method. A calls it the bad infinity and says it cancels itself, as Nero burning Rome shows.
- Getting tied down. Friendship, marriage and office each bind you, so you can no longer rotate. A keeps an exit in every relationship.
- Losing control of memory and hope. Crop Rotation says to throw hope overboard, because hope stops you limiting yourself, and to remember and forget at will. The Unhappiest One portrays the people who failed at this: the rememberer stuck in a past he never had, the hoper fixed on what he knows cannot happen, and the unhappiest one in whom both cross.
- The link to state outright: boredom and unhappiness are not the same thing. Boredom is the emptiness A's method fights, and unhappiness is being absent from yourself. Failing the method leaves you absent in the past or the future. A also says those who bore themselves can end by dying of boredom or shooting themselves out of curiosity.

### Q: Exam 1, question 2: what is A's position, and do you agree?
**Topic:** The aesthetic life-view  **Lec:** exam1  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 6f448284
**A:** Key:
1. A's position is that life is judged by how it feels to live it, and his three essays give a recipe for satisfaction.
2. The recipe: expect the worst (The Unhappiest One), keep no lifelong ties (Crop Rotation), treat your fortune as outside your control (Ancient Tragedy).
3. Against the baseline: suffering survived has meaning only if it is real and yours, and "life is misery" gives that up in advance.
4. Against no ties: B says a life of moods has no self, and that marriage is where first love lasts.
5. Against fate as comfort: it gives up responsibility for your own deeds, which B says is what having a self means.

State the position before judging it. A judges life by how it feels to live it, and his three essays give a recipe for satisfaction: expect the worst (The Unhappiest One), keep no lifelong ties (Crop Rotation), and treat your fortune as outside your control (Ancient Tragedy). Then disagree with one reason per part.
- Against the baseline: suffering survived has meaning only if it is real and yours, and a baseline of "life is misery" gives that up in advance.
- Against no ties: B says a life of moods has no self, and that marriage is where first love lasts.
- Against fate as comfort: it gives up responsibility for your own deeds, which B says is what having a self means. A's own words admit the modern person is in despair, which is where B's half of the book begins.

## Exam 2 — the real questions (Fri Oct 16, as Matt recalled them)

### Q: Exam 2, question 1: who is Mozart's character for the seeking stage?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** exam2  **Type:** recall  **Id:** fe17768d
**A:** Key:
1. It is Papageno, the bird-catcher in *The Magic Flute*.

Papageno, the bird-catcher in *The Magic Flute* (1791). Desire has woken and looks among the many but desires none of them yet; A's best word for it is "it discovers". Not Tamino, whom A calls an unmusical figure.

### Q: Exam 2, question 2: which Mozart opera is A's picture of the dreaming stage?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** exam2  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 0dc311c5
**A:** Key:
1. It is *The Marriage of Figaro*, through the Page, Cherubino.

*The Marriage of Figaro* (1786), through the Page, Cherubino. Desire has not yet separated from its object and longs for "the one" in the abstract. The aria Kraal played was "Voi che sapete".

### Q: Exam 2, question 3: what does A mean by saying the desired rests "androgynously" in the Page's desire?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** exam2  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 33141249
**A:** Key:
1. The desired is so little separated from desire that it rests inside the desire, and desire and its object are both neuter.
2. The image is male and female sharing one blossom in plant life.

In the first stage desire is so indeterminate, and its object so little separated from it, that the desired rests androgynously inside the desire, the way male and female share one blossom in plant life; desire and its object are joined and both neuter. A finds it fitting that the Page's part is set for a woman's voice, since that contradiction hints at the stage's own.

### Q: Exam 2, question 4: what does A say about music and the demonic?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** exam2  **Type:** recall  **Id:** f7d1387e
**A:** Key:
1. Music is the demonic: its absolute object is sensual genius, the sensual that Christianity shuts out and so posits as a principle.
2. So music is a Christian art.
3. The sensual takes the form of the demonic "in aesthetic indifference", when spirit has excluded it but not yet judged it.
4. Don Juan is the demonic specified as the sensual, and Faust the demonic specified as the spiritual.

Music's absolute object is sensual genius, the sensual that Christianity shuts out and, by shutting it out, posits as a principle; so music is a Christian art, and it is the demonic. The sensual takes the form of the demonic "in aesthetic indifference" when spirit has excluded it but not yet judged it; Don Juan is the demonic specified as the sensual, Faust the demonic specified as the spiritual. Kraal's chain: desire overpowers a person, being overpowered is the demonic, music is desire's medium, so music is demonic. Distractor to avoid, which Matt picked on the exam: that music inspires demonic deeds. A's claim is about what music expresses, not what it causes.

### Q: Exam 2, question 5: what does the number 1,003 mean in the Catalogue Aria?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** exam2  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 432cebe2
**A:** Key:
1. The number is odd and accidental, which suggests the list is by no means closed: Don Giovanni is still on the move.
2. 1,003 is the count for Spain on Leporello's list.
3. Heard as a force of nature in music, the number could be any number; pictured as one individual, it would be comic.

It is the count for Spain on Leporello's list. A says the number is odd and accidental, which gives the impression the list is by no means closed: Don Giovanni is still on the move, and his desire never ends. Pictured as a particular individual, 1,003 would be comic; heard as a force of nature in music, the number could just as well be any number.

## Lec 9 — The Musical Erotic: why music, and the three stages (Mon Oct 5, logged 2026-10-05)

### Q: Name the three immediate erotic stages in A's essay, in order.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** recall  **Id:** c53e85f9
**A:** Parts:
1. The first stage is desire dreaming.
2. The second is desire seeking.
3. The third is desire desiring.

Dreaming has not woken to any object and longs for "the one" in the abstract. Seeking has woken and looks among the many but desires none of them yet. Desiring wants each particular woman as an instance of woman as such. The order follows desire's growth.

### Q: Give the Mozart character and opera for each of A's three stages.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 6b4cbfdb
**A:** Parts:
1. Dreaming is the Page, Cherubino, in *The Marriage of Figaro*.
2. Seeking is Papageno, the bird-catcher, in *The Magic Flute*.
3. Desiring is Don Giovanni, in *Don Giovanni*.

The order follows desire's growth, not the dates: *Figaro* premiered in 1786, *Don Giovanni* in 1787 and *The Magic Flute* in 1791, so *The Magic Flute* is the latest opera but the middle stage.

### Q: What separates the first stage from the second? Answer with Cherubino and Papageno.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 4c32478a
**A:** Key:
1. In the first stage desire has not woken to any object and dreams; in the second it has woken and turned outward.
2. Cherubino's dreaming is a vague, melancholy longing, in love with love itself, "the one" in the abstract.
3. Papageno's desire notices the many possible objects and looks among them cheerfully, but does not yet desire any one of them.

In the first stage desire has not woken to any object. It dreams: a vague, melancholy longing, in love with love itself, "the one" in the abstract. In the second stage desire has woken and turned outward. It notices the many possible objects and goes looking among them, cheerfully, but does not yet desire any one of them; it seeks one it could desire (p. 90). Dreaming is inward and has no object; seeking is outward and aims at the many.

### Q: What is the third stage, desiring, and why does A treat it as the unity of the first two?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 6c419ce7
**A:** Parts:
1. In the third stage, Don Giovanni's, desire is fully itself and has a particular object.
2. It wants each particular woman as an instance of woman as such, so it never settles and never runs out.
3. It is the unity because it keeps the dreamer's pull toward the ideal and the seeker's turn to the many, and aims both at the particular.

In Don Giovanni desire is fully itself. It has a particular object, and it wants each particular woman as an instance of woman as such, so it never settles and never runs out. It keeps the dreamer's pull toward the ideal and the seeker's turn to the many, and aims both at the particular. That is why Don Giovanni is A's picture of the sensuous erotic genius.

### Q: Reconstruct the argument Kraal laid out for why music is the best expression of desire. Give each premise and the conclusion.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** derive  **Id:** 9cdd5bc0
**A:** Parts:
1. Desire is a mode of immediacy: it exists only in the moment.
2. Language is a mode of reflection: it comes after the event and describes it.
3. Painting and sculpture are modes of representation: they hold a figure still in space.
4. Music is a mode of immediacy: it exists only while it sounds, in time.
5. Conclusion: only music matches desire's mode, so music is desire's best, in fact its only adequate, expression.

First, desire is a mode of immediacy: it exists only in the moment, in passion, before any thought about it. Second, language is a mode of reflection: it comes after the event and describes it (p. 80). Third, painting and sculpture are modes of representation: they hold a figure still in space. Fourth, music is a mode of immediacy: it exists only while it sounds, in time. Conclusion: only music matches desire's mode, so music is desire's best, in fact its only adequate, expression.

### Q: In "desire is a mode of immediacy", what does "immediacy" mean, and why can language not capture it?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** apply  **Id:** cdcc4c81
**A:** Parts:
1. Immediacy is experience as it happens, before reflection steps back to name it, explain it or judge it.
2. Language works by reflection: to say something you stand outside the moment and describe it afterwards.
3. So putting desire into words already turns it into something past and mediated.

Immediacy is experience as it happens, before reflection steps back to name it, explain it or judge it. Language works by reflection: to say something you stand outside the moment and describe it afterwards, so putting desire into words already turns it into something past and mediated. Music does not stand outside the moment, because it is itself an event in time.

### Q: Why does A rank Mozart's *Don Giovanni* above every other classic work?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 55312a1d
**A:** Key:
1. In *Don Giovanni* the most abstract idea, desire in its immediacy, meets the most abstract medium, music.
2. For A a work is classic when its idea and its medium fit perfectly.
3. Music is the only medium that can carry the sensuous erotic, so *Don Giovanni* is the one work where they meet.

For A a work is classic when its idea and its medium fit perfectly. Desire in its immediacy, the sensuous erotic, is the most abstract idea, and music is the most abstract medium, the only one that can carry it. *Don Giovanni* is the one work where that idea meets that medium, so A puts it, and Mozart, at the top of the classics. The worshipful tone belongs to A, the aesthete.

### Q: In the passage Kraal read from page 75, what happens to the sensuous "in its mediate state", and why does A call music a Christian art?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 26dcd64c
**A:** Parts:
1. Once reflected, the sensuous comes under language and becomes subject to ethical categories: it is talked about and judged.
2. Music is a Christian art because Christianity set spirit against flesh and, by excluding the sensuous, first made it a principle of its own.

Once the sensuous is reflected, it comes under language and becomes subject to ethical categories: it is talked about and judged. In its immediacy it can be expressed only in music. Music is a Christian art because Christianity set spirit against the flesh, and in excluding the sensuous it made the sensuous a principle of its own for the first time; music is the art of that excluded principle.

### Q: True or false, and justify: A argues that painting and sculpture are the best media for the sensuous because they show beauty.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** critique  **Id:** cf5f3905
**A:** Key:
1. False: A gives the sensuous erotic in its immediacy to music alone, not to painting or sculpture.
2. Painting and sculpture are modes of representation that hold a figure still in space, which suits beauty at rest.
3. Desire is movement in time.

False. Painting and sculpture are modes of representation that hold a figure still in space. That suits beauty at rest, but desire is movement in time, so A gives the sensuous erotic in its immediacy to music alone.

### Q: Kraal compared A's essay to Nietzsche. Name the book and its year, the two principles, the kind of art that goes with each, and where music falls.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 349c5ce5
**A:** Parts:
1. The book is *The Birth of Tragedy*.
2. It dates from 1872.
3. The Apollonian goes with the plastic arts such as sculpture.
4. The Dionysian goes with the non-plastic art of music.
5. Music falls on the Dionysian side.

*The Birth of Tragedy*, 1872. The Apollonian goes with the plastic arts such as sculpture: order, form, rationality, and language as Kraal glossed it. The Dionysian goes with the non-plastic art of music: wild, orgiastic, pre-rational. Music falls on the Dionysian side, which lines up with A putting desire's immediacy in music rather than in sculpture or words. Nietzsche never read Kierkegaard, so the parallel is not influence.

### Q: "A's desire is just hedonism." Is that right?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 5aea426b
**A:** Key:
1. Not quite: A's desire comes before any theory, and Don Giovanni does not weigh pleasures, he simply desires.
2. Hedonism is the view that pleasure is the good, already reflective: a theory with reasons and a policy of chasing pleasant states.
3. A's desire need not even feel good, since the first stage is melancholy.
4. The calculating pursuer of enjoyment is a different figure, the reflective seducer of "The Seducer's Diary".

Not quite. Hedonism is a view that pleasure is the good, so it is already reflective, a theory with reasons behind it and a policy of chasing pleasant states. A's desire comes before any theory: Don Giovanni does not weigh pleasures, he simply desires, and desire need not even feel good, since the first stage is melancholy. The calculating pursuer of enjoyment is a different figure, the reflective seducer of "The Seducer's Diary" at the end of A's papers.

### Q: Which essay of *Either/Or* is "The Musical Erotic", who wrote it, and where does it sit among A's papers?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 4526d815
**A:** Parts:
1. Its full title is "The Immediate Erotic Stages or The Musical Erotic".
2. It is by A, the aesthete, in Part I of *Either/Or*.
3. It is the second of A's papers, right after the Diapsalmata.

Its full title is "The Immediate Erotic Stages or The Musical Erotic". It is by A, the aesthete, in Part I of *Either/Or* (1843), the papers Victor Eremita published. It is the second of A's papers, right after the Diapsalmata.

### Q: Which aria did Kraal play for the first stage, who sings it, and which line shows desire that has no object yet?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** recall  **Id:** a577215b
**A:** Parts:
1. The aria is "Voi che sapete".
2. It is sung by Cherubino, the Page, in *The Marriage of Figaro*.
3. The line is "Ricerco un bene fuori di me, non so chi il tiene, non so cos'è".

"Voi che sapete", sung by Cherubino, the Page, in *The Marriage of Figaro*. The line is "Ricerco un bene fuori di me, non so chi il tiene, non so cos'è": I search for a good outside myself; I don't know who holds it, I don't know what it is. He longs without any particular woman in view, which is A's first stage, desire dreaming.

### Q: According to Kraal, what role do the three operas play in "The Musical Erotic"? Are they A's proof that only music expresses desire?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 4418bca6
**A:** Parts:
1. He uses them to clarify the three stages of desire.
2. No: A does not use the operas to prove that only music expresses desire.

No. A does not use the operas to prove his argument. He uses them to clarify the three stages of desire. What justifies that choice is his claim that music and desire are both expressions of immediacy (Kraal, email of Oct 6).

### Q: The operas have words, and A says language is reflection. How would A answer that, according to Kraal, and whom does A praise?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 21174560
**A:** Parts:
1. A would say the words are heuristic, a help, rather than essential to what the music expresses.
2. The sign is that A praises Mozart, the composer, and not the librettists.

Kraal grants that the words create a prima facie problem. A would say the words are heuristic, a help, rather than essential to what the music expresses. The sign is that A praises Mozart, the composer, and not the librettists Da Ponte (*Figaro*, *Don Giovanni*) and Schikaneder (*The Magic Flute*).

### Q: True or false, and justify: in "The Musical Erotic", A argues that the aesthetic life must be built on sensuous desire.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** critique  **Id:** e133db64
**A:** Key:
1. False: A is not saying the aesthetic life is built on desire.
2. The desire-driven life he studies is one kind of aesthetic life.
3. It is aesthetic because what is desired is beauty, and aesthetics is concerned with the beautiful.

False. Kraal: A is not saying the aesthetic life is built on desire. He is saying that the desire-driven life he studies is one kind of aesthetic life, because what is desired is beauty, and aesthetics is concerned with the beautiful.

### Q: Does A see himself as a living example of the immediacy he praises in Don Giovanni?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** 9  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 50717b83
**A:** Key:
1. Not fully: A does not take himself to be as paradigmatic an example of immediacy as Don Giovanni.
2. A makes various attempts at this immediacy, which the Seducer's Diary will show.

Not fully. Kraal: A makes various attempts at this immediacy, which the Seducer's Diary will show, but he does not take himself to be as paradigmatic an example as Don Giovanni.

## Lec 10 — missed; the first two stages, from the reading (Wed Oct 7, written 2026-10-09)

### Q: In A's first stage, how do desire and its object relate?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** 10  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 6b7bdd00
**A:** Key:
1. Desire and its object are not yet separate.
2. The object exists only as something dreamt inside the desire, so the desire does not know what it wants.

They are not yet separate. The object exists only as something dreamt inside the desire, so the desire does not know what it wants. That is why the stage is called dreaming.

### Q: What mood does A give the first stage, and what mood the second?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** 10  **Type:** recall  **Id:** d271db69
**A:** Parts:
1. The first stage, Cherubino's, is quiet and melancholy, a longing with no aim.
2. The second stage, Papageno's, is cheerful and lively.

The first stage, Cherubino's, is quiet and melancholy, a longing with no aim. The second, Papageno's, is cheerful and lively.

### Q: In the second stage the object of desire separates from desire. In what form does it appear, and what does desire then do?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** 10  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 9981355c
**A:** Parts:
1. The object appears outside desire, as the many: all the women in the world.
2. Desire turns outward and looks among them, seeking one it can desire, but does not yet desire any one of them.

It appears outside desire, as the many: all the women in the world. Desire turns outward and looks among them, but does not yet desire any one of them; it seeks one that it can desire (p. 90).

### Q: True or false, and justify: A's three stages follow the order in which Mozart wrote the operas.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** 10  **Type:** critique  **Id:** b2a82fca
**A:** Key:
1. False: the order is the growth of desire, not the order in which Mozart wrote the operas.
2. Dreaming is *Figaro* (1786), seeking is *The Magic Flute* (1791), and desiring is *Don Giovanni* (1787).

False. The order is the growth of desire: dreaming (*Figaro*, 1786), seeking (*The Magic Flute*, 1791), desiring (*Don Giovanni*, 1787). *The Magic Flute* is the latest opera but the middle stage.

### Q: Which character from *The Magic Flute* does A take as the picture of the second stage: the hero Tamino or the bird-catcher Papageno?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** 10  **Type:** recall  **Id:** b98a97e4
**A:** Key:
1. It is Papageno, the bird-catcher, not the hero Tamino.

Papageno, the bird-catcher. In his first aria he wants a net to catch girls by the dozen, which is desire looking among the many.

## Lec 11 — Don Giovanni: desire for woman in general (Fri Oct 9, logged 2026-10-09)

### Q: In Mozart's *Don Giovanni*, who sings the Catalogue Aria, what is the catalogue, and how many women does it list for Spain?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** 11  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 48c526d2
**A:** Parts:
1. Leporello, Don Giovanni's servant, sings it to Donna Elvira.
2. The catalogue is his list of the women his master has loved, country by country.
3. It lists 1,003 for Spain.

Leporello, Don Giovanni's servant, sings it to Donna Elvira. The catalogue is his list of the women his master has loved, country by country: 640 in Italy, 231 in Germany, 100 in France, 91 in Turkey, and 1,003 in Spain.

### Q: What did Kraal take Leporello's catalogue to show about desire at the third stage?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** 11  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 2a71c663
**A:** Key:
1. The list is never closed: there is always room for another name.
2. So desire at the third stage cannot be satisfied: each woman won only points to the next.

The list is never closed. There is always room for another name, so desire at the third stage cannot be satisfied: each woman won only points to the next.

### Q: According to the Catalogue Aria, what is Don Giovanni's "dominant passion"?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** 11  **Type:** recall  **Id:** d3130ed9
**A:** Key:
1. His dominant passion is the young beginner ("la giovin principiante"), a young, inexperienced girl.

The young beginner ("la giovin principiante"), a young, inexperienced girl. The same aria says he conquers even old women for the pleasure of adding them to the list, and does not care whether a woman is rich, ugly or beautiful.

### Q: What does Don Giovanni desire in each woman, according to A, and why does it not matter to him whether she is rich, ugly or beautiful?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** 11  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 98a1c99c
**A:** Key:
1. He desires what she has in common with every other woman, the feminine or womanhood in general.
2. Since what he wants is the same in all of them, the differences between women do not matter to him.

He desires what she has in common with every other woman, the feminine or womanhood in general, not anything extraordinary about her. Since what he wants is the same in all of them, the differences between women do not matter to him, and his desire has the same force for each one.

### Q: Kraal called Don Giovanni a tragedy for the women. Explain why.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** 11  **Type:** apply  **Id:** a71ac5c2
**A:** Key:
1. His desire was never for her as a person but for womanhood in general, so each woman is replaceable.
2. The only difference that counts for him is whether she has been seduced yet.
3. She believed it was about her and finds out only afterwards.

Each woman is the object of his desire, but the desire was never for her as a person; it was for womanhood in general. So each is replaceable, and the only difference that counts for him is whether she has been seduced yet. She stays the same person while he moves on, and the tragedy is that she believed it was about her and finds out only afterwards.

### Q: True or false, and justify: Don Giovanni uses desire as a cure for boredom, the way Crop Rotation recommends.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** 11  **Type:** critique  **Id:** bd742b3a
**A:** Key:
1. False: Don Giovanni plans nothing; he simply desires, which is what makes him immediate.
2. Crop Rotation's cure is a reflective technique.
3. Crop Rotation rejects the extensive method of chasing ever more new things, which is what an endless list of new women looks like.

False. Don Giovanni plans nothing; he simply desires, which is what makes him immediate. Crop Rotation's cure is a reflective technique, and it rejects the extensive method of chasing ever more new things, which is what an endless list of new women would look like.

### Q: Who wrote "The Seducer's Diary"? Give A's claim, Eremita's suspicion and Kraal's view.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** 11  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 63c77e1c
**A:** Parts:
1. A presents it as a diary written by Johannes that he only found and copied.
2. Eremita, in the Preface, suspects A wrote it himself.
3. Kraal treats A as the Diary's author and reads it as A's own attempts at the immediacy he praises in Don Giovanni.

A presents it as a diary written by Johannes that he only found and copied. Eremita, in the Preface, suspects A wrote it himself. Kraal treats A, the author of "The Musical Erotic", as the Diary's author, and reads the Diary as A's own attempts at the immediacy he praises in Don Giovanni.

### Q: True or false, and justify: in A's account, Don Giovanni seduces by planning and deceiving.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** 11  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 71e4b65a
**A:** Key:
1. False: Don Giovanni does not really seduce in that sense; he desires, and the desire itself works seductively.
2. Seduction by plan is reflective and belongs to Johannes, the seducer of the Diary.

False. A says Don Giovanni does not really seduce in that sense: he desires, and the desire itself works seductively. Seduction by plan is reflective, and belongs to the seducer of the Diary, Johannes.

### Q: Kraal traced desire from wanting someone, to wanting one among a set, to wanting the particular. Match each to its stage, character and opera.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** 11  **Type:** apply  **Id:** b4a0de1d
**A:** Parts:
1. Wanting someone in the abstract is stage 1, dreaming: Cherubino in *The Marriage of Figaro*.
2. Wanting one among a set is stage 2, seeking: Papageno in *The Magic Flute*.
3. Wanting the particular is stage 3, desiring: Don Giovanni in *Don Giovanni*.

Wanting someone, in the abstract, is stage 1, dreaming: Cherubino in *The Marriage of Figaro*. Wanting one among a set is stage 2, seeking: Papageno in *The Magic Flute*. Wanting the particular is stage 3, desiring: Don Giovanni in *Don Giovanni*.

## Reading: "The Musical Erotic" — the introduction, and things that belong together (pp. 52–55 of the PDF, written 2026-10-13)

### Q: What is the opening section of "The Musical Erotic" called, and which four pairs does A give of things that belong together?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 11294247
**A:** Parts:
1. The opening section is the "Platitudinous Introduction".
2. Axel goes with Valborg, the pair of lovers.
3. Homer goes with the Trojan War.
4. Raphael goes with Catholicism.
5. Mozart goes with Don Juan.

It is the "Platitudinous Introduction", and A refers back to it by that name later, when he says the sensual in its immediacy can be expressed only in music. It opens with the happy Greek view that calls the world a cosmos, an orderly whole. A says the same wisdom rules in the world of ideals, joining together things that belong together: Axel with Valborg, the pair of lovers; Homer with the Trojan War; Raphael with Catholicism; and Mozart with Don Juan.

### Q: True or false, and justify: A says it was an accident, a lucky meeting of forces, that Mozart got Don Juan as his subject.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** critique  **Id:** af1c90e9
**A:** Key:
1. False: A attacks that view and calls it a wretched unbelief.
2. For A the union of Mozart and Don Juan is good fortune, which is not the same as the fortuitous.
3. A says the view comforts mediocre minds, because it lets them believe their lack of fame is a mistake of fate.

False. That is the view A attacks and calls a wretched unbelief. It holds that lovers find each other by accident, since a hundred other girls would have done as well, and that many a poet would be as immortal as Homer, or many a composer as Mozart, if only they had got the chance. A says this view comforts mediocre minds, because it lets them believe their lack of fame is a mistake of fate, and he calls it a convenient optimism that every high-minded soul finds repugnant. For A the union of Mozart and Don Juan is good fortune, which is not the same as the fortuitous.

### Q: How many factors does the fortuitous have, according to A, and how many does good fortune have? Use his Homer example.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 346a4b7d
**A:** Key:
1. The fortuitous has one factor, and good fortune has two.
2. The fortuitous is only the accident that the most remarkable epic theme, the Trojan War, fell to Homer's lot.
3. Good fortune puts the accent on Homer as much as on the material that came to him.

The fortuitous has one factor: the accident that the most remarkable epic theme imaginable, the Trojan War, fell to Homer's lot. Good fortune has two: that the most remarkable epic material came to Homer, with the accent on Homer as much as on the material. A calls historical fortune a divine conjunction of historical forces, and says this harmony is what sounds in every work we call classic. So too with Mozart: it was good fortune that perhaps the only true musical subject was granted to Mozart.

### Q: A admits that insisting on Mozart's first place is childish. How does he describe himself, and what does he threaten to do if the church refuses him?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** fec0aaeb
**A:** Parts:
1. A says he is like a young girl in love with Mozart and must have him placed highest whatever the cost.
2. He will petition the deacon, the priest, the dean, the bishop and the whole consistory.
3. If they refuse, he will leave the congregation and form a sect that accepts no one besides Mozart.

Everyone in the small band of immortals is equally high, because infinitely high, so arguing over first and last place is childish, like squabbling over one's place in line at confirmation. Still, A says he is like a young girl in love with Mozart and must have him placed highest whatever the cost. He will petition the deacon, the priest, the dean, the bishop and the whole consistory. If they refuse, he will leave the congregation and form a sect that not only places Mozart highest but accepts no one besides Mozart. He says Mozart cost him his reason and is the only pillar keeping everything from collapsing into chaos for him. Only then does he offer a considered proof of Mozart's claim.

### Q: In the introduction, what makes a work classic, and what goes wrong if you stress only Homer's subject or only Homer's poetic activity?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 4f5e3386
**A:** Parts:
1. In a classic, matter and form cohere so absolutely that a later reflective age can hardly separate them even in thought.
2. Stressing only Homer's subject forgets that the subject reaches us only through Homer's grasp of it, transformed by him.
3. Stressing only his poetic activity forgets that the poem could not be what it is unless its form was the material's own.

In a classic the two forces, matter and form, cohere so absolutely that a later reflective age can hardly separate them even in thought without a misunderstanding. Stress only Homer's luck in getting the best epic subject, and you forget that the subject reaches us only through Homer's grasp of it, transformed by him. Stress only his poetic activity, and you forget that the poem could never have been what it is unless its form was the material's own. The poet wants his material, and wanting rightly is a gift, the mystery of genius, like a divining rod that wants only where what it wants is present. A notes that one school of aesthetics stressed form one-sidedly, odd since its members followed Hegel, who stressed the subject matter.

### Q: Which single work makes Mozart a classic composer, according to A? How does Homer's *Batrachomyomachia* help him argue that one work is enough?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 27f13f5e
**A:** Parts:
1. *Don Giovanni*, which A calls Mozart's acceptance piece, the work that wins him admission among the immortals.
2. Usually a single work stamps an artist as a classic, and Homer's *Batrachomyomachia*, the battle of the frogs and mice, did not.
3. That is not because its subject is trivial: the balance of matter and form is what makes a classic.
4. If the artist alone made a work classic, everything he produced would be classic, the way a bee always builds the same cell.

*Don Giovanni*, which A calls Mozart's acceptance piece, the work that wins him admission among the immortals. Usually a single work, or a single set of works, stamps an artist as a classic. Homer also wrote the *Batrachomyomachia*, the mock-epic battle of the frogs and mice, and it did not make him a classic. That is not because its subject is trivial: the balance of matter and form is what makes a classic. If the artist alone made a work classic, everything he produced would be classic, the way a bee always builds the same cell, and saying he was just luckier with one work answers nothing. So lumping all Mozart's works together as equally great does him no service. All classics rank equally, because each ranks infinitely high, so any ordering of them has to rest on something inessential, which leads to A's rule for sorting them into classes.

## Reading: "The Musical Erotic" — ranking the classics, and why only music (pp. 56–60 of the PDF, written 2026-10-13)

### Q: What rule does A use to sort the classic works into classes, and why does he call the rule "contingent"?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 833145fe
**A:** Parts:
1. The more abstract, and so the poorer, the idea and the medium, the less likely the work can ever be repeated.
2. The more concrete, and so the richer, the idea and the medium, the more likely further works of the same kind will follow.
3. He calls the rule contingent because it only counts how many works a class can hold.
4. It does not say one classic is better than another, since every classic is equally perfect.

The more abstract, and so the poorer, the idea and the medium, the less likely the work can ever be repeated: once it has found its expression, it has it once and for all. The more concrete, and so the richer, the idea and the medium, the more likely it is that further works of the same kind will follow. He calls the rule contingent because it only counts how many works a class can hold. It does not say one classic is better than another, since every classic is equally perfect. Before this he rejects sorting the classics by subject matter, by form, or by how rich the medium is, because in a classic the idea and the form cannot be pulled apart.

### Q: According to A, what makes an idea concrete, and what makes a medium concrete? Which medium is the most concrete, and which is the most abstract?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** f51c452d
**A:** Parts:
1. An idea becomes concrete by being filled with history.
2. A medium is concrete to the degree that it comes close to language.
3. Language is the most concrete medium of all.
4. The most abstract medium is the one farthest from language: architecture.

An idea becomes concrete by being filled with history. A medium is concrete to the degree that it comes close to language, and language is the most concrete medium of all. The most abstract medium is the one farthest from language: architecture.

### Q: How does A use sculpture and Homer to illustrate his rule for sorting the classics?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 051168af
**A:** Parts:
1. Sculpture's idea is entirely abstract, with no relation to history, and its medium is abstract too.
2. So the class of classic sculptures will hold only a few works.
3. Homer's epic has a concrete idea and language as its medium, so the class of classic epics can hold several equally classic works.

Sculpture's idea is entirely abstract, with no relation to history, and its medium is abstract too, so the class of classic sculptures will hold only a few works. Homer's epic has a concrete idea and language as its medium, so the class of classic epics can hold several works that are all equally classic, because history keeps supplying new epic material.

### Q: Architecture has the most abstract medium. Why does A not put it at the top, and what does he rank the classics by instead?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 66401fd5
**A:** Parts:
1. Architecture's medium is the most abstract, but its ideas are closely tied to history, more closely than sculpture's are.
2. So the most abstract medium does not always carry the most abstract idea.
3. A ranks the classics by how abstract the idea is, not the medium.

The most abstract medium does not always carry the most abstract idea. Architecture's medium is the most abstract, but its ideas are closely tied to history, more closely than sculpture's are. So A chooses to rank by how abstract the idea is, not the medium.

### Q: The most abstract idea, for A, is sensuous genius ("the spirit of sensuality" in the PDF). Why can it not be expressed in sculpture, in painting or in poetry, and why can music express it?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** d4a91ed2
**A:** Parts:
1. Not sculpture, because sensuous genius is a quality of inwardness and sculpture shows outer form.
2. Not painting, because it exists in a succession of moments, and only what exists in a single moment can be painted.
3. Not poetry, because although a succession of moments sounds epic, it has not reached the level of words; it stays immediate.
4. Music can express it because music has time in it, yet it does not express the historical.

Not in sculpture, because it is a quality of inwardness, and sculpture shows outer form. Not in painting, because it has no fixed outline: it is energy, storm, impatience and passion, existing in a succession of moments rather than in a single moment, and only what exists in a single moment can be painted. Not in poetry, because although a succession of moments sounds epic, it has not reached the level of words; it stays immediate. Music can express it because music has time in it, yet it does not express the historical.

### Q: Why will Mozart never have a rival, according to A? Use his comparisons with Goethe's *Faust* and with Greek sculpture.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 1488a614
**A:** Key:
1. In *Don Giovanni* the most abstract idea meets the most abstract medium, so it can be expressed only once; a rival could only rewrite it.
2. Goethe's *Faust* is a classic, but its idea is historical and its medium is language, so every significant age will have its own *Faust*.
3. *Don Giovanni* is unique like the classic Greek sculptures, but its idea is even more abstract, so music has only one such work.

In *Don Giovanni* the most abstract idea meets the most abstract medium, so it can be expressed only once; any composer who tried to compete could only write *Don Giovanni* again. Goethe's *Faust* is a genuine classic, but its idea is historical and its medium is language, so every significant age will have its own *Faust*. *Don Giovanni* stays the only one of its kind, like the classic sculptures of Greece. Its idea is even more abstract than sculpture's, which is why there are several classic sculptures but only one such work in music. A later adds (p. 95) that writing a Don Juan after Mozart would be like writing a post-Homeric *Iliad*.

### Q: True or false, and justify: A puts *Don Giovanni* first among the classics because it is Mozart's greatest masterpiece, the most perfect work of art.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** critique  **Id:** fd4338e0
**A:** Key:
1. False: for A every classic is equally perfect, so the ranking is not about quality.
2. *Don Giovanni* comes first because its idea, sensuous genius, is the most abstract idea there is.
3. Music is the only medium for that idea, so it is the one work of its kind and can never be repeated.

False. For A every classic is equally perfect, so the ranking is not about quality. *Don Giovanni* comes first because its idea, sensuous genius, is the most abstract idea there is, and music is the only medium for it, so it is the one work of its kind and can never be repeated. Calling it a magnum opus misses that point.

### Q: Right after ranking *Don Giovanni* first among the classics, what does A admit about his own argument?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 530ef0bb
**A:** Key:
1. He admits he has made almost no attempt to prove that *Don Giovanni* is a classic at all; he simply assumes it.
2. He drops the inquiry and says it was written only for those in love, like a lovers' quarrel over nothing.
3. He says the rest of the essay will throw light on it along the way.

He drops the inquiry and says it was written only for those in love, like a lovers' quarrel over nothing. He admits he has made almost no attempt to prove that *Don Giovanni* is a classic at all. He simply assumes it, and says the rest of the essay will throw light on it along the way.

## Reading: "The Musical Erotic" — the stages and Don Juan (pp. 61–100 of the PDF, written 2026-10-13)

### Q: According to A, under which category did sensuality exist in Greece, and under which did Christianity first posit it? What changed between the two?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 30bbefda
**A:** Parts:
1. In Greece sensuality existed under the category of soul.
2. Christianity posited sensuality under the category of spirit, as the thing spirit must exclude.
3. In Greece it was opposed to nothing and lived in harmony with the soul, so it was an element, not a principle.
4. Being excluded is what made it a principle, a power and a system in its own right.

In Greece, and in paganism generally, sensuality existed under the category of soul. There it was opposed to nothing; it lived in harmony with the soul in the beautiful individual, so it was an element, not a principle. Christianity posited it under the category of spirit, as the thing spirit must exclude, and being excluded is what made it a principle, a power and a system in its own right. That is why A says Christianity brought sensuality into the world and, in the same breath, that Christianity drove it out. The objection that sensuality existed before Christianity misses the point, because what is excluded always exists before what excludes it.

### Q: Which Greek god does A use to show that the Greeks never concentrated love in one individual, and what is odd about him?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** a59b5a9b
**A:** Key:
1. The god is Eros.
2. He was the god of love but was not himself in love, apart from one exception, and had far fewer affairs than other gods or men.
3. Those who felt love referred its power to him, but the power lay in them, not in him.

Eros. He was the god of love but was not himself in love, apart from one exception, and he had far fewer affairs than the other gods or men. Gods and men who felt love referred its power to him, but the power lay in them, not in him. A calls this the converse of representation and of the Incarnation, where the whole fullness of life is gathered in one individual and others share it only by looking at him. Christianity brought the idea of representation into the world. So the sensual erotic as a principle concentrated in one individual, which A calls the spirit of the sensual erotic, is an idea the Greeks never had.

### Q: What does A mean by calling music "the demonic", and what is music's absolute object? Give his parallels for sculpture and painting.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** a215f4b7
**A:** Parts:
1. Music is the medium for the sensual that Christianity shuts out and so posits, which makes it a Christian art and the demonic.
2. Music's absolute object is the sensual erotic genius.
3. Sculpture's absolute object is human beauty.
4. Painting's absolute object is heavenly transfigured beauty.

Music is the medium for what Christianity shuts out and, by shutting it out, posits: the sensual. In that sense music is a Christian art, and it is the demonic. Its absolute object, its proper object, is the sensual erotic genius. Music can express other things too. In the same way sculpture can show more than human beauty, though human beauty is its absolute object, and painting more than heavenly transfigured beauty, though that is its absolute object. A's rule is to look for each art's concept and ignore what else it can do, just as man's concept is spirit even though he can also walk on two legs, and language's concept is thought.

### Q: A admits he is a layman about music. From which neighbouring "country" does he approach it, and why is music a language only in a certain sense?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 88d5a161
**A:** Parts:
1. He approaches music from language, walking along its border to trace the outline of the land next door.
2. Music is a language in that it is a spiritually qualified medium, the one that what spirit excludes needs to assert itself.
3. Language is the one absolutely spiritually qualified medium and the proper medium for the idea, so music ranks below it.

He says he has no expertise and stands outside music, at most a "proselyte of the gate", a convert who gets no further than the outer gate. He approaches music from language, the country he knows, by walking along its border to trace the outline of the land next door. Music is called a language because it is a spiritually qualified medium: once spirit is posited, what spirit excludes needs such a medium to assert itself, and that medium is music. But language is the one absolutely spiritually qualified medium and the proper medium for the idea, so music ranks below it. In language the sensual is reduced to a mere instrument and constantly negated: someone who heard the movements of the tongue or the vibrations of the air instead of the words, or saw each letter while reading, would speak, hear or read badly. In sculpture and painting the sensual is part of the work itself, which shows that these media, like nature and architecture, are dumb.

### Q: Which two features does music share with language and with no other medium, according to A? Why does music existing only in performance not count against it?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** e30b09e0
**A:** Parts:
1. Both address the ear, which A calls the most spiritually determined of the senses.
2. Both have time as their element, while every other medium has space as its element.
3. Existing only in performance looks like a flaw, but A takes it as proof that music is a higher, more spiritual art.

First, both address the ear, which A calls the most spiritually determined of the senses. Nature also reaches the ear, but only as pure sound, which is why a cow mooing or a nightingale singing says nothing. Second, both have time as their element, while every other medium has space as its element; even a murmuring brook's time is time in a spatial form. Music exists only while it is performed, since read from the score it exists only in an unreal sense. That looks like a flaw beside the arts whose works last in space, but A takes it as proof that music is a higher, more spiritual art.

### Q: True or false, and justify: A argues that music is a richer and more perfect medium than language, because music begins where language ends.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 812b7a15
**A:** Key:
1. False: taking music as the richer medium is a misunderstanding, like valuing "ugh!" above a whole thought.
2. Music marks the limit of language at both ends: going up through oratory, verse and rhyme, and going down to interjections and babbling.
3. Language cannot express the immediate because reflection kills the immediate, and that apparent poverty is language's wealth.
4. That the immediate cannot be pinned down is a defect, not a perfection.

False. Going up through language, from prose through oratory, verse and rhyme, A arrives at music; going down, from prose to interjections and a child's babbling, he arrives at music again. So music marks the limit of language at both ends, coming first and last, but taking it as the richer medium is a misunderstanding, like valuing "ugh!" above a whole thought. Language cannot express the immediate because reflection kills the immediate. That apparent poverty is language's wealth: the immediate cannot be pinned down, and that is a defect, not a perfection. For the same reason A has no sympathy for pure music that thinks it can do without words.

### Q: A divides the spiritually qualified immediate into two kinds. Which kind is music's absolute object, and how does that explain why music belongs to the Christian era?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 168fb484
**A:** Parts:
1. Music's absolute object is immediacy outside the spiritual, sensual immediacy, which only music can express, never language.
2. Since spirit excludes sensual immediacy, it belongs to Christianity, so music belongs to the Christian era, not antiquity.

Immediacy that falls inside the spiritual can be expressed in music, but for it music is only a prelude that is always cancelled, because it has to become spirit and so be expressed in language. Immediacy that falls outside the spiritual is sensual immediacy. It can be expressed only in music, never in language, so it is music's absolute object. Since spirit excludes it, it belongs to Christianity, which explains why music was not properly developed in antiquity and belongs to the Christian era. A backs this with religious zealots: the stronger the religiosity, the more music is given up for the word, and a Presbyterian in a story by Achim von Arnim calls the organ the devil's bagpipes.

### Q: True or false, and justify: A's three stages are separate levels of consciousness, each existing on its own, that desire passes through one after another.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 2fdc79f8
**A:** Key:
1. False: together the stages make up one immediate stage, not separate levels.
2. A warns against taking "stages" literally and says "metamorphoses" might fit better.
3. The first two are like predicates revealed one at a time that fall into the last, real stage, and alone exist only in concept.
4. They are not levels of consciousness, because even the last stage has not reached consciousness.
5. A treats them separately only because Mozart's music gives each its own expression.

False. A warns against taking "stages" literally and says "metamorphoses" might fit better. Together they make up one immediate stage. The first two are like predicates being revealed one at a time, which all fall into the last stage, the real one, and on their own they exist only in concept. They are not levels of consciousness either, because even the last stage has not reached consciousness. A treats them separately only because Mozart's music gives each its own expression. Seen from the third stage, the first two are one-sided presentiments of Don Giovanni, each hinting at one side, which is why for the third stage A takes the whole opera at once instead of singling out one part.

### Q: In the first stage, why is desire melancholy even though it already has its object? Give A's image of the painted ceiling and what he makes of the Page being sung by a woman.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** apply  **Id:** cab33074
**A:** Parts:
1. Desire possesses what will become its object without having desired it, so in another sense it does not possess it.
2. The pain comes from too much, not too little: the object is so close it is inside the desire, which cannot get to desiring.
3. A ceiling covered with figures presses down while a single figure makes it seem higher, like desire and object at the first and a later stage.
4. The Page's part is written for a woman's voice, fitting because the object rests androgynously in the desire, like male and female in one blossom.

Desire possesses what will become its object, but without having desired it, so in another sense it does not possess it. The pain comes from too much, not too little: the object is so close that it is inside the desire, and desire cannot get to the point of desiring. Its one definite feature is that it is infinitely deep, like Thor drinking from a horn whose other end is the ocean. A ceiling covered with figures presses down on you, while a single figure makes it seem higher; that is how desire and its object relate at the first stage and at a later one. The Page's part is written for a woman's voice, which A finds fitting: the object is so little separated that it rests androgynously in the desire, like male and female in a single blossom, so both are neuter.

### Q: A links the Page to Don Giovanni twice. Give both links and the moment in *Figaro* each rests on.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** e615abca
**A:** Parts:
1. First, teased by Susanna about loving Marcellina too, Cherubino answers only "She is a woman", since his object is womanliness.
2. A calls this the perfect analogy to Don Giovanni gladly adding even sixty-year-old coquettes to his list.
3. Second, when Cherubino jumps out of the window, Susanna exclaims that he will have luck with the girls.
4. A says the Page is the future Don Giovanni, meaning the mythical Page once he breaks free, not just by growing older.

First, when Susanna teases Cherubino for being in love with Marcellina as well, his only answer is "She is a woman." For the mythical Page, Marcellina matters as much as the Countess, because his object is womanliness, which both share. A calls this the perfect analogy to Don Giovanni gladly adding even sixty-year-old coquettes to his list, except that Don Giovanni's desire is far more intense and definite. Second, when Cherubino jumps out of the window, Susanna exclaims that he will have luck with the girls. A says the Page is the future Don Giovanni, meaning the mythical Page once he breaks free and gets going, not that he becomes Don Giovanni just by growing older.

### Q: A sums up Mozart's music for each stage in a short phrase. Give his phrase for the Page, for Papageno and for Don Giovanni's force, and the predicate he gives sensual genius itself.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** a1d1c9a3
**A:** Parts:
1. The Page: "drunk with love".
2. Papageno: "merrily chirping, vigorous, sparkling with love".
3. Don Giovanni's force: "exuberant good cheer".
4. Sensual genius itself: "It sounds."

The Page: "drunk with love", the kind of intoxication that presses joy into an opaque dejection rather than making it transparent. Papageno: "merrily chirping, vigorous, sparkling with love", heard above all in his first aria and the chime of bells. Don Giovanni's force: "exuberant good cheer". Sensual genius, which never develops but rages on as if in a single breath: "It sounds."

### Q: What is A's best description of the second stage, and how does Don Giovanni go beyond it? In A's one-line summary, who picks out, who enjoys and who checks?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 15662e41
**A:** Parts:
1. A's best description of the second stage is "It discovers."
2. Don Giovanni is a knight who goes out to conquer ("veni, vidi, vici"), and for him discovery and conquest are the same thing.
3. He forgets the discovery in the conquest and leaves it to his servant Leporello, who keeps the list.
4. Papageno picks out, Don Giovanni enjoys, Leporello checks.

"It discovers." Seeking desire looks for what it could desire but does not yet desire it, and its delight is in discovering the many. Don Giovanni's journey is more than a voyage of discovery: he is a knight who goes out to conquer ("veni, vidi, vici", I came, I saw, I conquered). For him discovery and conquest are the same thing; he forgets the discovery in the conquest and leaves it to his servant Leporello, who keeps the list. Papageno picks out, Don Giovanni enjoys, Leporello checks.

### Q: True or false, and justify: A counts *The Magic Flute* a classic opera, because its plot raises the erotic to married love.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: the three stages  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 4dc80e7d
**A:** Key:
1. False: for all its perfect single numbers, A does not count *The Magic Flute* a classic.
2. The opera tries to give the erotic a deeper ethical meaning, which takes it beyond what music can express.
3. Its goal, ethically qualified or married love, is absolutely unmusical.
4. Tamino is not a musical figure at all: his flute fails to have any effect.

False. The opera tries to give the erotic a deeper ethical meaning, which takes it beyond what music can express, so for all its perfect single numbers it is not a classic. The whole work is biased towards consciousness, and its goal, ethically qualified or married love, is absolutely unmusical. Tamino, the hero, is not a musical figure at all: his flute, which gives the opera its name, fails to have any effect, and his playing is only a way to pass time and drive away thought. Papageno's duets with Pamina and Papagena also fall outside the immediately musical. A blames the lines on either Schikaneder or the Danish translator and calls them mostly crazy and stupid; Papageno calling himself a child of Nature is his example.

### Q: To which age does A assign the Don Juan idea? Give the pairs he lists to show how that age represented its ideas.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** dabb8596
**A:** Parts:
1. The Don Juan idea belongs to Christianity and, through Christianity, to the Middle Ages: A places it in the later Middle Ages, a little before Faust.
2. The king has his fool.
3. Faust has his Wagner.
4. Don Quixote has Sancho Panza.
5. Don Giovanni has Leporello.

Nobody knows when the idea arose, but it belongs to Christianity and, through Christianity, to the Middle Ages. The Middle Ages embody the idea of representation: a whole is represented in one individual, but only one side of life at a time. These representative figures often come in pairs, the second one comic, making up for the first one's outsized greatness. The king has his fool, Faust has his Wagner, Don Quixote has Sancho Panza, and Don Giovanni has Leporello. A places Don Juan in the later Middle Ages, when spirit and flesh had grown into absolute opposition, a little before Faust.

### Q: What is the Venusberg in A's essay, who is its first-born, and is it the realm of sin?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** a35e9180
**A:** Parts:
1. The Venusberg is the kingdom of sensuality, a mountain the Middle Ages tell of that is on no map.
2. Its first-born is Don Juan.
3. It is not yet the realm of sin, because A takes it in aesthetic indifference: excluded by spirit but not yet judged.
4. Once reflection enters it shows itself as the realm of sin, but then Don Juan is slain and the music stops.

It is a mountain the Middle Ages tell of that is on no map. It is the kingdom of sensuality, where language, sober thought and reflection have no home and all you hear is the elemental voice of passion. Its first-born is Don Juan. It is not yet the realm of sin, because A takes it at the moment it appears in aesthetic indifference: excluded by spirit, but not yet judged or condemned. That is the sensual in the form of the demonic. Once reflection enters it shows itself as the realm of sin, but then Don Juan is slain and the music stops.

### Q: How does A contrast Don Juan with Faust? Give his formula for each, and say which of the two has a legend.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 7e3375ad
**A:** Parts:
1. Don Juan is the demonic specified as the sensual.
2. Faust is the demonic specified as the spiritual that the Christian spirit excludes.
3. Faust survives in a legend; Don Juan has no real legend, only an old comic booth-theatre piece.
4. Don Juan's idea stayed mysterious until it found its proper medium, music, because the sensual cannot be conceived in an individual.
5. The Faust idea is more concrete, which is why every generation can have a new Faust.

Both are Titans of the Middle Ages. They differ from the ancient Titans by standing alone, with all the power gathered in one individual. Don Juan is the demonic specified as the sensual; Faust is the demonic specified as the spiritual that the Christian spirit excludes. Faust survives in a legend. Don Juan has no real legend, only an old comic booth-theatre piece, probably his origin, in which he was a braggart who imagined he had seduced every girl. A's explanation is that the idea stayed mysterious until someone found its proper medium, music. Faust is an idea that is also essentially an individual, while the sensual cannot be conceived in an individual. The Faust idea is also more concrete, which is why it has been reinterpreted many times and every generation can have a new Faust.

### Q: A says Don Giovanni hovers between idea and individual. What does that mean, and what happens to the number 1,003 if you picture him as one particular man?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 42402d6e
**A:** Parts:
1. He hovers between being idea, meaning energy and life, and being an individual, and that hovering is the vibration of music.
