2. Pictured as one particular man, it becomes comic that he has seduced 1,003, and attention shifts to whom he seduced and how.

He hovers between being idea, meaning energy and life, and being an individual, and that hovering is the vibration of music. He is like the shapes that foam makes in a stormy sea: an image that keeps appearing but never gets form or substance. Picture him as a particular man you can see or hear speak, and it becomes comic that he has seduced 1,003, and attention shifts to whom he seduced and how. In music he is a force of nature that no more tires of seducing than the wind tires of raging, so the number could be anything. A still commends one feature of 1,003: it is odd and accidental, which suggests the list is not closed and Don Giovanni is still on the move.

### Q: True or false, and justify: Hercules, who had his way with fifty daughters in a single night, is A's example of a Greek seducer.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** critique  **Id:** f56338d0
**A:** Key:
1. False: A says the idea of a seducer is entirely absent from Greek consciousness, and Hercules is no seducer.
2. Greek love is of the soul, so by its own lights it is faithful: loving many is accidental.
3. Only sensual love is faithless by its own lights.
4. Don Giovanni loves not one but all, so he seduces all, because sensual love exists only in the moment.

False. A says the idea of a seducer is entirely absent from Greek consciousness, and Hercules is no seducer however long his list. Greek love is of the soul, not sensual, so by its own lights it is faithful: loving many is accidental, and while in love with one the Greek does not think of the next. Chivalrous love is also of the soul, and faithful for the same reason. Only sensual love is faithless by its own lights. Don Giovanni loves not one but all, which means he seduces all, because sensual love exists only in the moment and the moment is the sum of moments.

### Q: Love of the soul is dialectical in two ways, A says. Name both, and say what Don Giovanni's love has instead.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** apply  **Id:** b3a3ea7d
**A:** Parts:
1. First, love of the soul carries doubt about whether it will be happy and returned.
2. Second, love of the soul differs with each person loved, and that is its wealth.
3. Don Giovanni's love has no doubt, since he is always victorious, and no time for difference: he sees her and loves her in the same moment, and in that moment it is over.
4. So love of the soul is a continuation in time and sensual love is a disappearance in time.

First, love of the soul carries doubt and unease about whether it will be happy and returned; even Jupiter doubts his victory. Don Giovanni has no such doubt, since he is always victorious, and A calls that an impoverishment, not an advantage. Second, love of the soul differs with each person loved, and that is its wealth. Don Giovanni has no time for that: seeing her and loving her happen in the same moment, in that moment it is all over, and the same thing repeats endlessly. So love of the soul is a continuation in time and sensual love is a disappearance in time. Music is the medium for that disappearance, because it is more abstract than language and expresses the general in the concreteness of immediacy.

### Q: Why does A call the Catalogue Aria Don Giovanni's real epic? Use his thought experiment with the poet.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** apply  **Id:** c3d58d95
**A:** Key:
1. It is epic because, though short, it can go on as long as needed, since it can always start again.
2. The most gifted poet with total command of language would never finish the list, nor say half of what Mozart says in it.
3. Mozart gives a few large shapes, not the many women, because music is too abstract to express differences.
4. What we hear is the voice of sensuality, not Don Giovanni as an individual or his speech.
5. His life is a sum of moments with no coherence, so he can be epic only by constantly finishing and starting over.

Give the most gifted poet who ever lived total command of language and set him to sing the list of the seduced: he would never finish, and even if he did he would not say half of what Mozart says in this one piece. Mozart does not go into the many women at all; he gives a few large shapes, because music is too abstract to express differences. So the musical epic is short, yet it has the epic quality of going on as long as needed, since it can always start again. What we hear is not Don Giovanni as an individual, or his speech, but the voice of sensuality, heard through the longing for femininity. His life is a sum of moments that repel each other and have no coherence, so the only way he can be epic is by constantly finishing and starting over.

### Q: Why is it right, for A, that the seduction we see on stage is of Zerlina, a peasant girl? And why is Donna Elvira dangerous to Don Giovanni?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 8fb68479
**A:** Parts:
1. For Don Giovanni every girl is an ordinary girl, because he desires what she shares with every woman.
2. Mozart kept Zerlina as insignificant as possible; a spiritual seducer would need a harder task than a peasant girl.
3. Elvira is dangerous to him because she has been seduced, not because she is uncommon.
4. Seduction lifts a woman into a higher sphere, a consciousness Don Giovanni himself lacks.

Mozart kept Zerlina as insignificant as possible, and critics who call her an uncommon girl misunderstand him. If Don Giovanni were a spiritual seducer, a little peasant girl would be a serious fault in the opera, since he would need a harder task. But for him every girl is an ordinary girl and every affair an everyday story, because he desires what she shares with every woman. Elvira is a danger to him not because she is uncommon but because she has been seduced. Seduction lifts a woman into a higher sphere, a consciousness Don Giovanni himself lacks, and Zerlina becomes dangerous in exactly the same way once she is seduced.

### Q: Which word would A rather use for Don Giovanni than "seducer", and why? Name the two kinds of time and the one power that Don Giovanni lacks.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 4859cbb9
**A:** Parts:
1. A would rather say "deceiver", because that word is more ambiguous.
2. A seducer needs reflection and consciousness, which Don Giovanni lacks, so the power of sensuality itself deceives the women.
3. He lacks the time ahead in which to lay plans.
4. He lacks the time behind in which to become conscious of what he did.
5. He lacks the power of speech, and once given it he stops being musical.

Deceiver, because that word is more ambiguous. A seducer needs reflection and consciousness, and only then can one talk of cunning and intrigue. Don Giovanni lacks them, so it is the power of sensuality itself that deceives the women, more a kind of Nemesis than a plan. He lacks the time ahead in which to lay plans and the time behind in which to become conscious of what he did. He also lacks the power of speech, and once he is given it he stops being musical. The word needs care for a further reason: Don Giovanni is not too good for it, but he does not fall under ethical categories at all.

### Q: How old does A suggest Don Giovanni is, and why does he say that dwelling on his looks spoils everything?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** d6483f23
**A:** Parts:
1. He is handsome and not altogether young; if A must name an age, thirty-three, the age of a generation.
2. Dwelling on his looks loses the whole, as if he seduced through good looks, and then one sees him and no longer hears him.
3. Don Giovanni is not to be seen but heard.

Handsome and not altogether young; if A must name an age, thirty-three, the age of a generation. He offers this only as a concession. Dwelling on details like these loses the whole, as if Don Giovanni seduced through his good looks, and then one sees him and no longer hears him. Don Giovanni is not to be seen but heard, and A ends the section by telling the reader simply to listen to Mozart's *Don Giovanni*. The spoken word is no part of him either, since speech would at once make him a reflective individual.

### Q: Which adaptation does A take as the type of all the non-musical Don Juans? Why do a ballet and Byron's poem fall short too?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** a05b4d16
**A:** Parts:
1. Molière's *Don Juan*, a comedy older than Mozart's opera, is the type of all the non-musical Don Juans.
2. Once Don Juan speaks, the reflection behind his words pulls him out of the obscurity in which he can be heard only in music.
3. A ballet can show only the pangs of despair, which Don Juan shares with many, and not his essential, inward passion.
4. Byron gives him no spoken lines but tells his childhood and youth, which makes him a reflective personality and costs his ideality.

Molière's *Don Juan*, a comedy older than Mozart's opera; A says that judging it judges all the others. The turning point is spoken lines. Once Don Juan speaks, the reflection behind his words pulls him out of the obscurity in which he can be heard only in music. A ballet avoids lines and wisely keeps to the final scene, but pantomime can show only the pangs of despair, which Don Juan shares with many other desperate people, and not his essential passion, because Don Juan is something inward. Byron gives him no spoken lines but still uses words: he tells Don Juan's childhood and youth from ordinary life circumstances, which makes him a reflective personality and costs him the ideality of the traditional figure. A also faults Byron's poem for expanding epically.

### Q: Contrast the musical Don Juan with the reflective one. Which is the extensive seducer and which the intensive one, what does each enjoy, and how many must each seduce?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 69bc9234
**A:** Parts:
1. The musical Don Juan is the extensive seducer.
2. The reflective, self-aware Don Juan is the intensive seducer.
3. The musical Don Juan enjoys the satisfaction and has to seduce 1,003, winning each at a single stroke.
4. The reflective Don Juan enjoys the deception and the cunning, and needs to seduce only one.

The musical Don Juan is the extensive seducer. He wins his object at a single stroke, a flick of the wrist, he enjoys the satisfaction, and he has to seduce 1,003; obstacles only inflame him, and his victory is certain. The reflective, self-aware Don Juan is the intensive seducer. He enjoys the deception and the cunning, and he needs to seduce only one, so what interests us is how he does it: every small step counts, like a conjuring trick. Once Don Juan is an individual struggling against real obstacles, he falls in the category of the interesting. Faust, a later version of Don Giovanni who has the category of spirit, fits the second type: he seduces only one girl but destroys her far more intensely, and the power of such a seducer is speech, which means lies. "The Seducer's Diary", at the end of A's papers, is a seducer of this second kind.

## Reading: "The Musical Erotic", the last part (pp. 100–117 of the PDF, skimmed by Claude 2026-10-13)

### Q: Why, according to A, can only a ghost stop Don Giovanni, and what does he call the Commendatore's return?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** afbae89e
**A:** Parts:
1. Don Giovanni is sensual immediacy, and the negation of immediacy is spirit, so only a spirit, a ghost, can coerce him.
2. Don Giovanni can beat every obstacle, but you cannot kill a ghost.
3. A calls the Commendatore's return "a monstrous irony".

Don Giovanni is sensual life in its immediacy, and the negation of immediacy is spirit. No power on earth can coerce him, so only a spirit, a ghost, can. A calls the Commendatore's return "a monstrous irony", because Don Giovanni can beat every obstacle but you cannot kill a ghost, and irony is the taskmaster of the immediate life. In Molière the statue is a dramatic stumbling block instead, and modern comedy would simply put Don Juan in a debtor's gaol.

### Q: Why does Leporello's bond to his master make sense in the opera, when Sganarelle's does not in Molière?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** f23f6a2b
**A:** Key:
1. In the opera Don Giovanni is a force, and Leporello is absorbed by an obscure sympathy he cannot see through, so he cannot tear himself away.
2. That sympathy is what makes Leporello a musical figure.
3. In Molière Don Juan is a particular individual, so there is no rational ground for Sganarelle to stay when he is not even paid.

In the opera Don Giovanni is a force, and Leporello is drawn to him and absorbed by an obscure sympathy he cannot see through; that is what makes Leporello a musical figure, and why he cannot tear himself away. In Molière Don Juan is a particular individual, so Sganarelle relates to him as to an individual, and there is no rational ground for his staying when he is not even paid.

### Q: True or false, and justify: A takes the remark that Molière's Don Juan is more moral than Mozart's as a fair criticism of the opera.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** critique  **Id:** e08b533d
**A:** Key:
1. False: properly understood, A says, the remark praises the opera.
2. In the opera Don Giovanni is a seducer, not just talked about as one, and the music is seductive, which is its greatness.
3. The opera's aspiration is extremely moral and its impression salutary, because everything in it is big and has genuine pathos.

False. Properly understood, A says, the remark praises the opera. In the opera Don Giovanni is a seducer, not just talked about as one, and the music is seductive, which is its greatness. The opera's aspiration is extremely moral and its impression salutary, because everything in it is big and has genuine pathos. Calling it immoral is the foolishness of people caught by details who cannot grasp a whole.

### Q: How does A contrast opera with drama in their unity, their pace and the kind of action each can hold?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 0f7a1c0f
**A:** Parts:
1. Drama's unity is a thought, and opera's unity is a mood.
2. Drama hurries toward the fall, while opera lingers, expands in time and space, and moves horizontally.
3. Opera is not reflective enough for character and action, and expresses unreflective, substantial passion instead.
4. An action done with a conscious purpose cannot be set to music, so the action in an opera can only be immediate action.

Drama's unity is a thought: everything is converted into action and situation, and it hurries toward the fall. Opera's unity is a mood: it lingers, expands in time and space, and moves horizontally. Opera is not reflective enough for character and action and expresses unreflective, substantial passion instead. An action in the strict sense, a deed done with a conscious purpose, cannot be set to music, so the action in an opera can only be immediate action.

### Q: Which figure does A call the opera's "common denominator", what are the other figures, and who is the one exception to his power?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** bd1d573b
**A:** Parts:
1. Don Giovanni is the opera's common denominator.
2. The others are passions he posits: Elvira loves him, Anna hates him, Zerlina fears him, and Ottavio and Masetto go along for brotherhood-in-law.
3. The exception is the Commendatore, who is consciousness, so Don Giovanni has no power over him.

Don Giovanni. The others are not characters but passions he posits: Elvira loves him, Anna hates him, Zerlina fears him, and Ottavio and Masetto go along for the sake of brotherhood-in-law, so all are in his power. A's image is a solar system, the others lit only on the side turned toward him. The exception is the Commendatore, who is consciousness, so Don Giovanni has no power over him. He is kept in the background and appears only twice; the second time "he speaks no more, he judges".

### Q: What makes Elvira's first aria a musical situation rather than a dramatic one, for A?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 48d325bc
**A:** Key:
1. Don Giovanni stays in the background, unseen even by the audience, and his jeering laughter is flung into the pause in her aria.
2. We are meant to hear him inside Elvira, since her passion, love's hatred, is posited through him.
3. If both were characters, the unexpected encounter would be the situation and the staging would be a mistake.

Don Giovanni stays in the background, unseen even by the audience, and his jeering laughter is flung into the pause in her aria, so her passion, which A calls love's hatred, blazes up again. We are meant to hear him inside Elvira, since her passion is posited through him. If both were characters, the unexpected encounter would be the situation and the staging would be a mistake, because two characters cannot harmonize that way.

### Q: Which two arias does A say must go from *Don Giovanni*, and why?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** ce6532b2
**A:** Key:
1. Ottavio's and Anna's arias must go.
2. They are concert pieces rather than dramatic music, and Ottavio and Anna are too insignificant to justify holding up the action.

Ottavio's and Anna's. They are concert pieces rather than dramatic music, and Ottavio and Anna are too insignificant as figures to justify holding up the action. With those two removed, A says, the musical-dramatic pace of the opera is perfect as no other.

### Q: Which does A name as the most epic moment in the opera, and which as the most lyrical? Give his reason for each.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 66542671
**A:** Parts:
1. The most epic moment is Leporello's second aria, the list, sung to Elvira.
2. Leporello is the epic narrator of his master's whole life, and he forgets himself in Don Giovanni as he sings it.
3. The most lyrical moment is the champagne aria.
4. It has no dramatic situation, but in it Don Giovanni dissolves into music, "his life, foaming like champagne".

The most epic moment is Leporello's second aria, the list, sung to Elvira: Leporello is the epic narrator of his master's whole life, and he forgets himself in Don Giovanni as he sings it. The most lyrical moment is the champagne aria: it has no dramatic situation, but in it Don Giovanni dissolves into music, "his life, foaming like champagne", and the keynote of the opera sounds in it. The banquet in the finale is a situation rather than a lyrical moment, Don Giovanni driven to life's furthest point.

### Q: What does A say about the overture: when it should be composed, where its focus lies, and what mood Don Giovanni's life is born in?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** f32208f0
**A:** Parts:
1. An overture should be composed last, so that the composer is steeped in the music.
2. Its focus is Don Giovanni, not Don Giovanni and the Commendatore.
3. Don Giovanni's life is born in dread, a substantial dread that is his energy, and not in despair.

An overture should be composed last, so that the composer is steeped in the music; Mozart's is a perfect masterpiece, enough on its own to prove the opera a classic. Its focus is Don Giovanni, not Don Giovanni and the Commendatore: the deep voice of the opening grows weaker and is dragged into the race, and earnest returns only in the finale. Don Giovanni's life is born in dread, a substantial dread that is his energy and his demonic joy of life, and not in despair. The opera then rightly begins with Leporello, whose first aria belongs with the overture.

### Q: Name the three numbered sections of A's treatment of *Don Giovanni*, and the heading he gives the essay's closing page.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Don Giovanni  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 891e797c
**A:** Parts:
1. Section 1 is Sensual Genius Specified as Seduction.
2. Section 2 is Other Adaptations of Don Juan, Considered in Relation to the Musical Interpretation.
3. Section 3 is The Inner Musical Structure of the Opera.
4. The closing page is the Platitudinous Postlude.

1. Sensual Genius Specified as Seduction. 2. Other Adaptations of Don Juan, Considered in Relation to the Musical Interpretation. 3. The Inner Musical Structure of the Opera. The closing page is the Platitudinous Postlude, matching the Platitudinous Introduction, and it returns to Mozart's good fortune and to *Don Giovanni* as the highest of the classics.

## Reading: "Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern" (Sep 25–28; read by Claude 2026-10-13, frozen until the final)

### Q: Give the essay's full heading, who it is read before, and where it sits among A's papers.
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 01842e9e
**A:** Parts:
1. The full heading is "Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern. An Essay in the Fragmentary Endeavour".
2. It is read before the Symparanekromenoi.
3. It is the third of A's papers, right after "The Musical Erotic".

"Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern. An Essay in the Fragmentary Endeavour", read before the Symparanekromenoi, the same society A addresses in "The Unhappiest One". It is the third of A's papers, right after "The Musical Erotic". A describes the society's genre as the art of writing posthumous papers, which should feel like a ruin.

### Q: Which of Aristotle's terms does A use in the essay, and what does he take from each?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 8b2336c0
**A:** Parts:
1. Guilt (hamartia), which Aristotle requires the hero to have and A reads as lying between activity and passivity in Greek tragedy.
2. Thought (dianoia) and character (ethos) are the sources of tragic action.
3. The end (telos) is the main thing, so the characters are there for the sake of the action.
4. Fear and pity: A reads fear as the mood of the particular event and pity as the mood of the total impression.

Thought (dianoia) and character (ethos) as the sources of tragic action, with the end (telos) as the main thing, so that the characters are there for the sake of the action. Guilt (hamartia), which Aristotle requires the hero to have and which A reads as lying between activity and passivity in Greek tragedy. Fear and pity, the moods tragedy arouses: A reads fear as the mood of the particular event and pity as the mood of the total impression, which is why pity shares the dialectic of tragic guilt.

### Q: A says his age tends toward comedy. What two signs does he give, and what line sums up the second?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** e3d2576d
**A:** Parts:
1. The first sign is isolation.
2. The second sign is the flight from responsibility.
3. The line is "everyone wants to rule, no one wants the responsibility".

Isolation and the flight from responsibility. To be isolated is to assert oneself numerically, and the associations formed against isolation only confirm it, since a hundred asserting themselves as a hundred is the same move; A calls them infusoria in the body of a dissolving state, like the political clubs of Greece's decline. The age is melancholy enough to know what responsibility is and runs from it: "everyone wants to rule, no one wants the responsibility", his example being a French statesman who would take office only if the secretary of state were made responsible. The isolated individual is comic because he asserts his accidental individuality against necessity.

### Q: A calls the tragic a maternal love. What is the paternal love, what is the ethical, and what do the two kinds of leniency share?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 9d1cafab
**A:** Parts:
1. The paternal love is the religious: the ethical mollified by continuity.
2. The ethical is strict and harsh, like the judge who rules that the thief is a thief and not a thief's mother.
3. Continuity is also what gives the tragic its leniency.

The tragic has an infinite leniency: from the aesthetic side it is what divine love and mercy are, a maternal love that soothes. The ethical is strict and harsh, like the judge who rules that the thief pleading his mother's habits is a thief and not a thief's mother. The religious is a paternal love, the ethical mollified by continuity, which is also what gives the tragic its leniency. The aesthetic grants its pause before the opposition of sin is pressed home; the religious grants it only after. The age disdains both tragedy's tears and divine mercy.

### Q: What does A take from Hegel's *Aesthetics* on pity?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 4c9cc97b
**A:** Key:
1. A keeps Hegel's point that true tragic pity is sympathy at the same time with the sufferer's justification.
2. A sets aside Hegel's separation of ordinary compassion, about the finite side of suffering, as a misunderstanding.
3. A ties the kinds of compassion to the kinds of tragic guilt instead.

Hegel separates ordinary compassion, which concerns the finite side of suffering, from true tragic pity, and says true pity is sympathy at the same time with the sufferer's justification. A keeps the second point as "straight to the point", sets the first aside as a misunderstanding that applies to any tragedy, and ties the kinds of compassion to the kinds of tragic guilt instead.

### Q: True or false, and justify: A says remorse is the highest form of tragic pain.
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** critique  **Id:** f362370f
**A:** Key:
1. False: remorse is the bitterest pain, but it has an ethical reality, not an aesthetic one.
2. Its total transparency removes all aesthetic interest, and it refuses to be looked at, least of all by a spectator.
3. When the individual is left wholly to himself, his guilt is sin and his pain remorse, and the tragic is gone.

False. Remorse is the bitterest pain, but it has an ethical reality, not an aesthetic one. Its total transparency removes all aesthetic interest, and it refuses to be looked at, least of all by a spectator. The modern age works toward it as its highest tragic interest, which A puts with the age's other confusions: wanting to be edified in the theatre and influenced aesthetically in church. When the individual is left wholly to himself, his guilt is sin and his pain remorse, and the tragic is gone.

### Q: Which Greek play does A single out as a tragedy of suffering, and how does its hero differ from the modern Antigone?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** c8dad1af
**A:** Key:
1. A singles out Sophocles' *Philoctetes*.
2. Philoctetes complains that no one understands his pain, which A calls typically Greek; the modern Antigone wants no one to learn of hers.
3. His solitude on the island is an outward truth that makes his pain less; hers is figurative, and that unreality makes her pain real.

Sophocles' *Philoctetes*, Greek tragedy's own transition from sorrow to pain, with a high degree of reflection that objectivity still sustains: the hero rests in his unchangeable fate. Philoctetes complains that no one understands his pain, which A calls typically Greek and a deep human need. The modern Antigone does not want anyone to learn of her pain. His solitude on the island is an outward truth, which makes his pain less; her solitude is only figurative, and that unreality is what makes her pain real.

### Q: Complete A's formula: true tragic sorrow requires an element of what, and true tragic pain an element of what? Which needs transparency and which opaqueness?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 77f8dec2
**A:** Parts:
1. True tragic sorrow requires an element of guilt.
2. True tragic pain requires an element of innocence.
3. Sorrow requires transparency.
4. Pain requires opaqueness.

True tragic sorrow requires an element of guilt, true tragic pain an element of innocence. Sorrow requires transparency, pain opaqueness. The more innocence, the deeper the sorrow, but pressed to the limit this leaves aesthetics: the synthesis of absolute innocence and absolute guilt is metaphysical, which is why Christ's life is not called a tragedy.

### Q: Why does A make his modern tragic figure a woman?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 20e30a3d
**A:** Key:
1. A woman has the substantiality that sorrow needs to show itself.
2. As a member of a reflective world she has reflection enough to feel pain.
3. Her reflection must not be infinite, or it would refract her out of her inherited guilt and so out of sorrow.

A woman has the substantiality that sorrow needs to show itself, and as a member of a reflective world she has reflection enough to feel pain. Her reflection must not be infinite, or it would refract her out of her inherited guilt and so out of sorrow; as it is, it refracts her into her sorrow and turns it into pain at every moment.

### Q: A says anxiety is the modern tragic category. Give his two proofs that anxiety is reflection, and his three examples.
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** ec53095f
**A:** Parts:
1. From language: one is anxious "about something", so anxiety is separated from its object, whereas "my sorrow" can name what I sorrow over.
2. From time: one can be anxious only about the past or the future, which are reflective, never about the present.
3. Hamlet is tragic because he suspects his mother's guilt.
4. Robert le Diable asks how he came to cause so much evil.
5. Hogne, begotten by a troll, sees his shape in the water.

From language: one is anxious "about something", so the anxiety is separated from its object, whereas "my sorrow" can name what I sorrow over. From time: one cannot be anxious about the present, only about the past or the future, which are reflective, while Greek sorrow lives in the present tense. Anxiety both loves and fears its object, like an erotic glance. The examples are Hamlet, tragic because he suspects his mother's guilt; Robert le Diable, asking how he came to cause so much evil; and Hogne, begotten by a troll, seeing his shape in the water.

### Q: What does A's modern Antigone not know, and why does he call that the modern feature?
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** recall  **Id:** f649a732
**A:** Parts:
1. She does not know whether her father himself knew his guilt.
2. That ignorance inside her knowledge keeps her sorrow in motion and turns it into pain, the disquietude A calls the modern feature.

Whether her father himself knew his guilt. Oedipus is dead in A's version, honoured by the people; while he lived she could not confide in him, since he might not have known, and now confiding in anyone else would disgrace him. That ignorance inside her knowledge keeps her sorrow in motion and turns it into pain, which is the disquietude A calls the modern feature. Her public enthusiasm for her father is her only vent, and even that is pain, since anyone seeing her suffer might pick up the trail.

### Q: A sets the isolated individual a dilemma about tragic guilt. State both horns, and say what kind of dialectic joins family guilt to the individual instead.
**Topic:** Ancient Tragedy's Reflection in the Modern  **Lec:** reading  **Type:** derive  **Id:** 9de44b85
**A:** Parts:
1. Either the isolated individual is absolutely the creator of his own destiny, and nothing tragic remains, only evil.
2. Or individuals are mere modifications of the eternal substance of existence, and the tragic is lost again.
3. The dialectic that joins the guilt of the family to the individual is objective, not subjective.
4. Essentially it is piety: if one member suffers, all suffer, and the individual participates in the guilt.

Either the isolated individual is absolutely the creator of his own destiny, in which case nothing tragic remains, only evil, or individuals are mere modifications of the eternal substance of existence, and the tragic is lost again. Tragic guilt is inherited guilt, a substantial category like original sin, and the dialectic that joins the guilt of the family to the individual is objective, not subjective. Essentially it is piety, which A says cannot harm the individual: if one member suffers, all suffer, and the individual participates in the guilt.

## Lec 12 — the last lecture on The Musical Erotic: desire qua desire, Plato, Christianity and the demonic (Wed Oct 14, logged 2026-10-14)

### Q: Kraal gave the three parts of the soul from Plato's *Republic*. Name them, with Plato's own term for the middle one, and say what makes a person just and beautiful on that view.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Plato, Christianity and the demonic  **Lec:** 12  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 4c654ea4
**A:** Parts:
1. The first part is reason.
2. The middle part is drive, the spirited part, for which Plato's own term is thumos.
3. The third part is desire, appetite.
4. A person is just when the three are in balance under reason, with all three present.
5. On the Greek view the moral person is the beautiful one, and the beautiful person the moral one.

Reason, drive, which is Plato's spirited part (thumos), and desire, which is appetite. A person is righteous when the three are in balance under reason; all three must be present. On the Greek view the moral person is the beautiful one and the beautiful person the moral one.

### Q: Describe the chariot in Plato's *Phaedrus* as Kraal gave it. What does each part stand for, and what is the goal?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Plato, Christianity and the demonic  **Lec:** 12  **Type:** recall  **Id:** a67ed077
**A:** Parts:
1. The soul is a charioteer driving two horses, and the charioteer is reason.
2. The white horse is drive, Plato's spirited or noble part.
3. The black horse is desire, appetite.
4. The goal is to have the horses pull in perfect proportion so the person is balanced, not to kill the black horse.

The soul is a charioteer driving two horses. The charioteer is reason. The white horse is drive, Plato's spirited or noble part. The black horse is desire, appetite. The goal is to have the horses pull in perfect proportion, so the person is balanced. You do not kill the black horse; you bring it under balance.

### Q: Which Plato dialogue has the ladder from desiring one beautiful body up to the Form of Beauty, and how does that ladder run opposite to A's three stages?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Plato, Christianity and the demonic  **Lec:** 12  **Type:** apply  **Id:** eceba983
**A:** Parts:
1. It is the *Symposium*, in Diotima's speech.
2. Plato's ladder climbs by reason from one beautiful body to all bodies, to souls and knowledge, and finally to the Form of Beauty.
3. A's stages run the other way, from the abstract dream of "the one" through the many to the particular woman.
4. Every one of A's stages is immediate, with no reason in it.

The *Symposium*, in Diotima's speech. Plato's ladder climbs from the particular body to all beautiful bodies, to beautiful souls and knowledge, and finally to the abstract Form of Beauty, and the climb is the work of reason. A's stages go the other way, from the abstract dream of "the one" (Cherubino), through seeking among the many (Papageno), to the particular woman (Don Giovanni), and every stage is immediate, with no reason in it.

### Q: Contrast the Greek and the Christian attitudes to desire as Kraal set them out, and give A's claim about what Christianity did to the sensual.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Plato, Christianity and the demonic  **Lec:** 12  **Type:** recall  **Id:** c6e0bad1
**A:** Parts:
1. For the Greeks desire is one part of a balanced soul, nothing shameful, so they "would have smiled" at Don Giovanni.
2. For Christianity desire is sinful and to be reduced, so Christendom would not smile.
3. A's claim: by setting spirit against flesh, Christianity excluded the sensual and so first made it a principle of its own.

For the Greeks desire is one part of a balanced soul, nothing shameful, so they "would have smiled" at Don Giovanni. For Christianity desire is sinful and to be reduced, as many New Testament passages say, so Christendom would not smile. A's claim: by setting spirit against flesh Christianity excluded the sensual, and in excluding it made the sensual a principle of its own for the first time. That is why music, the art of that principle, is a Christian art and was not developed in antiquity.

### Q: Why did Kraal say music is "demonic" for A? Give the chain, and A's own formula for Don Juan and Faust.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Plato, Christianity and the demonic  **Lec:** 12  **Type:** derive  **Id:** 25bb5e9b
**A:** Parts:
1. Desire can overpower a person, being overpowered is what the demonic means, and music is desire's medium, so music is demonic.
2. Don Juan is the demonic specified as the sensual.
3. Faust is the demonic specified as the spiritual.

Desire can overpower a person; to be overpowered is what the demonic means; music is the medium of desire; so music is demonic. In A's text the sensual takes the form of the demonic "in aesthetic indifference" when spirit has excluded it but not yet judged it. Don Juan is the demonic specified as the sensual; Faust is the demonic specified as the spiritual.

### Q: What is the subtitle of *Don Giovanni*, how does the opera end, and what did Kraal say A does with that ending?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Plato, Christianity and the demonic  **Lec:** 12  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 8a654b79
**A:** Parts:
1. The subtitle is *Il dissoluto punito*, "The Rake Punished".
2. Don Giovanni is dragged down to hell by the statue of the Commendatore.
3. Kraal said A leaves the punishment out because it does not fit his view, cherry-picking the parts of the opera he likes.

*Il dissoluto punito*, "The Rake Punished", Kraal's "the punishment of the libertine"; the libretto is Da Ponte's, who had Dante's hell in mind. Don Giovanni is dragged down to hell by the statue of the Commendatore. Kraal said A leaves the punishment out because it does not fit his view, and cherry-picks the parts of the opera he likes.

### Q: True or false, and justify: A never mentions Don Giovanni's descent into hell.
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Plato, Christianity and the demonic  **Lec:** 12  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 9138cf91
**A:** Key:
1. False: A discusses the Commendatore's return and calls it "a monstrous irony".
2. Don Giovanni overcomes every obstacle, but you cannot kill a ghost; only spirit, the negation of immediacy, can coerce him.
3. Once reflection enters, the sensual realm appears as the realm of sin, Don Juan is slain and the music ends.
4. A keeps the ending but reads it aesthetically, as spirit ending immediacy, not as a moral lesson.

False as a claim about the text, though it is Kraal's view that A sets the punishment aside. A discusses the Commendatore's return in his section on the opera's structure and calls it "a monstrous irony": Don Giovanni overcomes every obstacle, but you cannot kill a ghost, and only a spirit can coerce him, because he is immediacy and spirit is its negation. Earlier A says that once reflection enters, the sensual realm appears as the realm of sin, Don Juan is slain and the music ends. A keeps the ending but reads it aesthetically, as spirit ending immediacy, not as a moral lesson.

### Q: What does "desire qua desire" mean, and what did Kraal mean by "there is no half and half"?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: Plato, Christianity and the demonic  **Lec:** 12  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 6e4e5a73
**A:** Parts:
1. Desire qua desire is desire taken purely as itself, with no reflection, logic or reasoning mixed in.
2. "No half and half" is A's "absolutely": sensual love is absolutely faithless and loves not one but all.
3. Don Giovanni is always absolutely victorious, without the doubt that love of the soul carries.

Desire taken purely as itself, with no reflection, logic or reasoning mixed in; that is what makes Don Giovanni immediate rather than a planning seducer. "No half and half" is A's "absolutely": sensual love is absolutely faithless and loves not one but all, and Don Giovanni is always absolutely victorious, without the doubt that love of the soul carries.

### Q: Kraal noted that older philosophers often misquoted, caring more for the idea a text inspired than for fidelity, and that A does the same with Mozart. How does A himself answer the charge that he reads into the music what is not there?
**Topic:** The Musical Erotic: why music  **Lec:** 12  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 6b52f84b
**A:** Key:
1. A answers that the reader will find in the music not only the little he presents but infinitely more.
2. He says the whole inquiry is "written only for those in love".

A admits the reader may doubt how far what he says is in Mozart's music and how far he reads it in, and answers that the reader will find in the music not only the little he presents but infinitely more. He says the whole inquiry is "written only for those in love". It fits Kraal's email point that A praises Mozart, not the librettists: what A takes from the operas is the idea the music gives him, not the text.

## Lec 13–14 — "The Seducer's Diary": Johannes and Cordelia (Mon Oct 19 and Wed Oct 21, logged 2026-10-25 from a classmate's shared notes)

### Q: Who is the seducer of "The Seducer's Diary", who claims to have found the diary, and what did Kraal say about who really wrote it?
**Topic:** The Seducer's Diary: Johannes and Cordelia  **Lec:** 13  **Type:** recall  **Id:** ac726101
**A:** Parts:
1. The seducer is Johannes, "Johannes the Seducer".
2. A says in his preface to the diary that he found it and copied it.
3. Kraal's view is that A wrote the diary himself, so Johannes is A's own creation and the facade is A acting a part.

The seducer is Johannes, "Johannes the Seducer". A says in his preface to the diary that he found it and copied it. Kraal's line, drawn as "Author A Facade" and "Real Author A", is that A wrote the diary himself, so Johannes is A's own creation and the facade is A acting a part.

### Q: What does Johannes call the highest enjoyment, and what did Kraal mean by calling his romance "counterfactual"?
**Topic:** The Seducer's Diary: Johannes and Cordelia  **Lec:** 13  **Type:** recall  **Id:** a109293f
**A:** Parts:
1. The highest enjoyment is to have someone desire you like a god, to make a woman fall absolutely in love with him.
2. It is counterfactual because the goal is to bring her to the point where she would give everything freely, not to get her to give it.
3. The feeling of being desired and needed absolutely is the prize, not the act.

The highest enjoyment is to have someone desire you like a god, to make a woman fall absolutely in love with him. It is counterfactual because the goal is not to get the girl to give everything but to bring her to the point where she would give everything freely. The feeling of being desired and needed absolutely is the prize, not the act.

### Q: True or false, and justify: Johannes wants Cordelia to give herself to him because he compels her.
**Topic:** The Seducer's Diary: Johannes and Cordelia  **Lec:** 13  **Type:** critique  **Id:** e04a0bb5
**A:** Key:
1. False: he wants a spiritual seduction in which she gives herself freely, with no ethical or moral compulsion.
2. "Only when it is free is it beautiful", so compulsion would spoil the enjoyment.
3. That is why he arranges things so that she breaks the engagement herself.

False. He wants a spiritual seduction in which she gives herself freely, with no ethical or moral compulsion, because "only when it is free is it beautiful". Compulsion would spoil the enjoyment, which is why he arranges things so that she breaks the engagement herself.

### Q: Name the stages of the Cordelia case as the lecture laid them out, in order.
**Topic:** The Seducer's Diary: Johannes and Cordelia  **Lec:** 13  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 6ccf8a26
**A:** Parts:
1. First comes observing: he notices her for the first time and is caught up in her details.
2. Second comes infatuation: his life develops meaning through her.
3. Third comes the plan, trap or strategy.
4. Fourth comes deception, which he justifies to himself.
5. It ends with the engagement she breaks and the "final enjoyment".

Observing (he notices her for the first time and is caught up in her details), infatuation (his life develops meaning through her), the plan, trap or strategy, then deception, which he justifies to himself. The affair ends with the engagement she breaks and the "final enjoyment".

### Q: Describe Kraal's two-Johannes diagram. What are the two figures, and what passes between each of them and Cordelia?
**Topic:** The Seducer's Diary: Johannes and Cordelia  **Lec:** 13  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 7126c911
**A:** Parts:
1. The upper figure is the facade, A acting a part, and it has direct communication with Cordelia.
2. The lower, dotted figure is the real A, doing the reflection and calculation, and it only watches her from behind the facade.

The upper figure is the facade, A acting a part, and it has direct communication with Cordelia. The lower, dotted figure is the real A, doing the reflection and calculation that tune the performance, and it only watches her from behind the facade. Kraal's phrase was "the mastermind behind the actor".

### Q: Set Johannes against Don Giovanni on one point: how does each of them seduce, and why does one of them need a diary?
**Topic:** The Seducer's Diary: Johannes and Cordelia  **Lec:** 13  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 72373ecf
**A:** Parts:
1. Don Giovanni is the immediate seducer: he desires and acts in the moment, which is why only music can present him.
2. Johannes is a reflective seducer: every move is planned, observed and adjusted.
3. Johannes needs a diary because his seduction unfolds in time and in prose, the form that fits a calculating mind.

Don Giovanni is the immediate seducer: he desires and acts in the moment, which is why only music can present him. Johannes is a reflective seducer: every move is planned, observed and adjusted, so his seduction unfolds in time and in prose, and a diary is the form that fits a calculating mind. The lecture's "reflection + calculation" is the whole difference.

### Q: How does Johannes justify deceiving Cordelia, in the lecture's two steps?
**Topic:** The Seducer's Diary: Johannes and Cordelia  **Lec:** 14  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 96433115
**A:** Parts:
1. First, he treats deception as the key to romantic love rather than a fault in it.
2. Second, in any romance someone is bound to be deceived, the girl or the man, so he might as well be the one acting.

First, he treats deception as the key to romantic love rather than a fault in it. Second, he argues that in any romance someone is bound to be deceived, either the girl deceives the man or the man the girl, so he might as well be the one acting. Kraal flagged the duplicity of this: the deception is of her and of himself.

### Q: Why does Johannes get engaged to Cordelia, and who ends the engagement?
**Topic:** The Seducer's Diary: Johannes and Cordelia  **Lec:** 14  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 104fc900
**A:** Parts:
1. He thinks engagements are silly because they go against the freedom romantic love needs, so the engagement is a step in the plan.
2. Cordelia breaks it off, which is what he wanted.

He thinks engagements are silly because they go against the freedom that romantic love needs, so the engagement is a step in the plan, not a commitment. Cordelia breaks it off, which is what he wanted, and she still comes back to him afterwards, which is the point of the design.

### Q: What is the paradox the lecture named at the end of the diary, and what follows from it for how the diary ends?
**Topic:** The Seducer's Diary: Johannes and Cordelia  **Lec:** 14  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 27471d97
**A:** Key:
1. Johannes wants Cordelia most while there is resistance in their love, and no longer wants her once she desires him totally.
2. So the "final enjoyment" is also the end of his interest, and the diary stops when the seduction is complete.

Johannes wants Cordelia most while there is resistance in their love and no longer wants her once she desires him totally. So the "final enjoyment" is also the end of his interest, and the diary stops when the seduction is complete. Conquest kills the thing he was after.

### Q: What did Kraal mean by the "meta-level" of inner and outer in the diary?
**Topic:** The Seducer's Diary: Johannes and Cordelia  **Lec:** 14  **Type:** recall  **Id:** b94e4c95
**A:** Key:
1. Everyone has an inner world of thoughts and an outer world in which others see only the physical person, and the two are not the same.
2. The diary shows the gap at its widest: Cordelia sees the facade, while the reader sees the calculating inner life.

Everyone has an inner world of thoughts and an outer world in which others see only the physical person, and the two are not the same. The diary shows the gap at its widest: Cordelia sees the facade, the reader sees the calculating inner life, and nothing in Johannes's outward conduct reveals it.

## Lec 15 — "Diapsalmata": moralizing and the meaning of life (Fri Oct 23, logged 2026-10-25 from a classmate's shared notes)

### Q: Why did Kraal say it is easy to moralize about Johannes, and what does moralizing presuppose?
**Topic:** Diapsalmata: moralizing and the meaning of life  **Lec:** 15  **Type:** recall  **Id:** e50bec8b
**A:** Parts:
1. It is easy because the diary presents Johannes as a bad man, so "how could he do this" comes naturally.
2. Moralizing presupposes a standard: definite views on how one should and should not live, a view of what a human life should be like.

The diary presents Johannes as a bad man, so "how could he do this" comes naturally. But moralizing presupposes a standard: definite views on how one should live and how one should not, in short a view of what a human life should be like. The Diapsalmata show that A has no such standard, so there is nothing in his own papers to moralize from.

### Q: What are the Diapsalmata, and what does the lecture read them as the voice of?
**Topic:** Diapsalmata: moralizing and the meaning of life  **Lec:** 15  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 16c2ca1c
**A:** Parts:
1. The Diapsalmata are the short aphorisms that open A's papers in Either/Or Part I.
2. The lecture reads them as the voice of someone who lacks a belief about the meaning of life.
3. He is disappointed with philosophy, has no clear moral view of what to do, and finds everything regrettable.

They are the short aphorisms that open A's papers in Either/Or Part I. The lecture reads them as the voice of someone who lacks a belief about the meaning of life: disappointed with philosophy, with no clear moral view of what you are supposed to do, for whom everything seems regrettable, and with only a very short philosophy of his own.

### Q: State Kraal's "digression" about discussing someone's thoughts, and say what it implies for A.
**Topic:** Diapsalmata: moralizing and the meaning of life  **Lec:** 15  **Type:** apply  **Id:** e542da1f
**A:** Parts:
1. Discussing someone's thoughts reduces them to beliefs that can never be objectively verified, so beliefs about how to live are subjective.
2. For A that means no one can tell him what to believe, and finding it out for himself is hard.
3. That is where his naive youthful confidence about the meaning of life was lost.

If you discuss someone's thoughts you reduce them to a series of beliefs that can never be objectively verified, so beliefs about how one should live, or the meaning of life, are subjective. For A that means no one can tell him what to believe, and finding it out for himself is hard, which is where his naive youthful confidence about the meaning of life was lost.

### Q: True or false, and justify: the lecture's point was that evolutionary biology gives the meaning of life, namely replicating your genes.
**Topic:** Diapsalmata: moralizing and the meaning of life  **Lec:** 15  **Type:** critique  **Id:** e62df3fb
**A:** Key:
1. False: reproduction is a precondition of life, and a precondition of life cannot be its meaning.
2. "You must replicate your genes" says what keeps the species going, not why you should live.

False. Reproduction is a precondition of life, and the lecture's point was that a precondition of life cannot be its meaning. "You must replicate your genes" says what keeps the species going, not why you should live, so the example shows what A is missing rather than supplying it.

### Q: Multiple choice. According to the lecture's map of the Diapsalmata, which of these is NOT something A says about the meaning of life? (a) It cannot be to die. (b) Perhaps only God knows it. (c) It is to serve others. (d) He knows what he wants even if he does not know what he knows.
**Topic:** Diapsalmata: moralizing and the meaning of life  **Lec:** 15  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 573259c4
**A:** Key:
1. The answer is (c): nothing on the map says the meaning of life is to serve others.

(c). The map has: it cannot be to die, it cannot come from a precondition or an evolutionary perspective, perhaps only God knows, he becomes disappointed with philosophy, he has no clear moral views and everything seems regrettable, he has a very short philosophy, and he knows what he wants even if he does not know what he knows. Nothing about serving others.

### Q: What did A once think was the best and most enjoyable thing, and how does that connect the Diapsalmata back to the diary?
**Topic:** Diapsalmata: moralizing and the meaning of life  **Lec:** 15  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 08246516
**A:** Parts:
1. A once thought romantic love was the best and most enjoyable thing.
2. For a while it was Cordelia: the seducer's absolute desire was A's one candidate for a meaning of life.
3. The diary shows that desire ending the moment it is satisfied, which is why the Diapsalmata voice has given up on it.

Romantic love, in the past. Kraal's note was "for a while it was Cordelia": the seducer's absolute desire was A's one candidate for a meaning of life, and the diary shows it ending the moment it is satisfied, which is why the Diapsalmata voice has given up on it. Johannes knows what he finds fun and enjoyable and pursues that, because he has no belief about what he should do.

## Lec 18 — "The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage": B's warning to A, and the ethical against the aesthetic (Mon Nov 2, logged 2026-11-02)

### Q: Who writes "The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage", to whom, in what form, and what does marriage stand for in the essay?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: B's task and tone  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** recall  **Id:** ba04eee5
**A:** Parts:
1. B, Judge William, writes it.
2. He writes it to A.
3. It is a letter.
4. Marriage stands for the ethical.

It is the first of B's two letters that make up Part II of *Either/Or*. B chose the letter form because it allows "admonitory and urgent tones" and keeps A from debating in general terms. Kraal's line was that if you play the game of life on the aesthetic's own parameter, the ethical still triumphs, because marriage turns out more aesthetic than romantic love.

### Q: What are the two things B sets out to show in "The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage"?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: B's task and tone  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 608d9bce
**A:** Parts:
1. That marriage is aesthetic, against A's view that it is unaesthetic and boring in the long run.
2. That the aesthetic can be sustained in marriage against life's obstacles, over time.

B prefixes each task with a short "polemical prologue" that answers A's sarcasm. He calls fighting for marriage his vocation as a husband, "pro aris et focis", for hearth and home.

### Q: How does B say he feels toward A, and what does he warn him about?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: B's task and tone  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 5a7b554f
**A:** Parts:
1. He loves A like a son, a brother and a friend.
2. He warns A about A himself, not about the world, and warns the world about A.
3. A is wasting his life: it will be nothing but approach-runs and a record of wasted possibilities.

B's own words are that he loves A "with the fear and trembling of a religious love", and "I am warning you not about the world but about yourself, and I am warning the world about you." Kraal's gloss: A has one chance at life, talents and responsibilities he should take up, and is on a self-destructive path. B would warn a daughter against A, and says A is not an enemy of marriage but only mocks and observes it.

### Q: Where did Kraal say the marriage vow comes from, and where does B say marriage belongs?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: B's task and tone  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 43a22f40
**A:** Parts:
1. Kraal: the vow comes from Christianity, around the year 1100.
2. B: marriage belongs to Christianity; the pagan nations and Judaism did not perfect it.

B grants the Orient its sensuality and Greece its beauty, but neither perfected marriage. The Church made marriage a sacrament in the twelfth century, which is the fact behind Kraal's date. In the ceremony the Church "exacts a vow", and the vow, the intention, gives marital love its law of motion.

### Q: Suppose, with A, that duty is the enemy of love. What follows for love, by B's argument, and what must A therefore give up?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: B's task and tone  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** apply  **Id:** 6fd7ade3
**A:** Key:
1. Duty always turns up eventually in a love relation, so if it is love's enemy, love cannot last and love loses: "love is not the true victor".
2. A, who makes love the highest thing, must therefore give up the premise that duty is love's enemy.

B's sentence: "If duty is the enemy of love, and love cannot defeat this enemy, then love is not the true victor." The argument is a trap for A: either deny that duty is love's enemy, or admit that his love loses and that he has disparaged it. B's own view is that duty is love's friend.

### Q: True or false, and justify: B concludes from his argument that one should live by duty and responsibility rather than by love.
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: B's task and tone  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 2c023bd9
**A:** Key:
1. False.
2. B's view is that duty is love's friend, not its rival: "duty here is just one thing, truly to love".

"You regard duty as the enemy of love, I regard it as its friend." Duty is not "one climate and love another"; it makes B's love "the true, temperate climate", and the aesthetic, the ethical and the religious are "the three great allies". Love held apart from duty is despair, which is why B rejects it, not because duty outranks love.

### Q: What distinguishes love from lust, according to B?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: first love and the reflective age  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 089d617e
**A:** Key:
1. Love has "the stamp of the eternal"; lust does not.
2. Lust is the sensual on its own, "the instantaneous", which at best makes the moment of pleasure into "a little eternity".

The true eternity in love needs "a determination of the will", which is the step from romantic to married love. Romantic love rests on the sensual and on beauty but is noble because of the consciousness of the eternal it takes up. B says A's "clever head" has conspired with his "lust", so that he renews the illusion of first love in new girls.

### Q: B quotes "do it or do not do it, you will regret both". Whose line is it, where is it from, and when does B say it is justified?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: marriage's history, habit and duty  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** recall  **Id:** a47ec4a3
**A:** Parts:
1. It is A's own line.
2. It is from the Diapsalmata.
3. B says it is justified only for someone who cannot see the aesthetic, the ethical and the religious as "the three great allies".

For such a person, B says, "life is without meaning". B calls it A's "pet theory" and uses it as the end point of taking duty to be love's enemy: that view is despair, whether A feels its pain or tries to forget it.

## Lec 19 — "The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage" continued: the ethical as the more aesthetic (Wed Nov 4, logged 2026-11-04 from a classmate's shared notes)

### Q: Kraal said B's major argument, that the ethical ends up more aesthetic than romantic love, goes back to Plato. What principle did he take from Plato, and how does it apply to marriage?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: the ethical as the more aesthetic  **Lec:** 19  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 8c654d9f
**A:** Key:
1. The beautiful derives its value from the good (Plato's *Republic*).
2. Marriage stands for the ethical, the good, so marriage ends up more beautiful than first love.

In *Republic* Book VI the Form of the Good is the most important thing to know, because just and beautiful things are beneficial only through their relation to it. The link to Plato is Kraal's; B does not name Plato in the assigned text. B's own words for the conclusion are that marriage is the "transfiguration" of first love and "even more beautiful than it", and that "really it is marriage that is poetic".

### Q: Kraal set the aesthetic against the ethical in two comparisons, "Children" and "Confinement". Give both sides of each, and the conclusion he drew.
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: the ethical as the more aesthetic  **Lec:** 19  **Type:** recall  **Id:** aff56207
**A:** Parts:
1. Children: dying childless and alone, against dying with the people you love around you.
2. Confinement: keeping your inner secrets shut in and relying on deception, against sharing your biggest secrets with your lover openly.
3. In each pair the first life is the aesthetic one and the second the ethical one, and the ethical one is the good.

Both pictures are Kraal's, built from B's letter. B's word for the confinement side is "secretiveness", and for the open side openheartedness, uprightness and "publicity". B does not use the deathbed picture as a reason to marry (next question).

### Q: True or false, and justify: B argues that one should marry so as to have children around one's deathbed.
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: the ethical as the more aesthetic  **Lec:** 19  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 6491137a
**A:** Key:
1. False.
2. The deathbed picture is Kraal's comparison of two lives, and marrying for children is one of the one-sided "whys" B rejects, since marriage must have no "why".

B quotes married people saying their children and grandchildren will "close our eyes" as their consolation, not as his argument. He still calls children "a blessing", with the poor woman in the square and her baby as his example. His warning about A's end is different: A's life will close with only the recollection of conscience and its record "of wasted possibilities".

### Q: In the "Confinement" comparison, why can a lover who keeps himself secret from his beloved not truly love, by B's reasoning?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: the ethical as the more aesthetic  **Lec:** 19  **Type:** derive  **Id:** 4411759b
**A:** Key:
1. Love is giving oneself, which means going out of oneself, while secretiveness wants to remain in itself.
2. Making an impression from a distance, so that the girl loves an image she made herself, is "not love but the coquetry of the seducer".

The one who loves has lost himself in the other, and in forgetting himself he is revealed to the other. So B calls openness "the life-principle of love" and inward secretiveness its death, and says it takes courage to reveal oneself as one truly is. The secrecy B keeps is toward the outside world, not between husband and wife.

### Q: Why, according to Kraal, does marriage represent the ethical?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: the ethical as the more aesthetic  **Lec:** 19  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 32c93814
**A:** Key:
1. Because it represents promise keeping.
2. The promise is the vow the Church exacts at the wedding.

"Promise keeping" is Kraal's phrase. B's own words for it are vow, intention and duty. The vow carries the intention, which gives married love a "law of motion" (a direction it develops in over time) and an inner history, and B says the language of duty is in the future tense, to suggest the historical. A's "no mishmash" objection attacks the same promise: what does it mean "to pledge oneself to love"? Kraal's Monday point that the vow comes from Christianity around 1100 belongs here.

### Q: Why, according to Kraal, does A not really love Cordelia, and whose test is he applying?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: the ethical as the more aesthetic  **Lec:** 19  **Type:** recall  **Id:** c6c02d42
**A:** Parts:
1. Their love is bound to a stretch of time, not eternal: it lasts only as long as the seduction.
2. The test is B's: what separates love from lust is "the stamp of the eternal".

The verdict on Cordelia is Kraal's. Cordelia is not in B's letter but in "The Seducer's Diary", which Kraal reads as A's own. B's lines on A fit it: A thinks any girl should count herself lucky to be his sweetheart "even for a week", and he renews first love by finding its illusion in new girls, which "betrays as much desperation as depravity". In the Diary, Johannes loses interest once Cordelia desires him completely.

### Q: Give the five steps of Kraal's diagram of marriage, in order.
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: the ethical as the more aesthetic  **Lec:** 19  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 8b847541
**A:** Parts:
1. The defining characteristic of marriage is love.
2. Love is not lust.
3. But love is not devoid of the sensuous, and the sensuous is not lust.
4. Love, its sensuous side included, carries a stamp of the eternal.
5. The change from romantic love into married (conjugal) love happens through the ethical will: intention and decision.

Each step is B's: marriage's "substance" is love; love differs from lust by "the stamp of the eternal"; first love is "not simply sensual"; the true eternity needs "a determination of the will". Two notes hang off the diagram: a civil marriage does not "require love" (at step 1), and romantic love seeks to become conjugal love (at step 4).

### Q: True or false, and justify: since love is not lust, B holds that married love must leave the sensual behind.
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: first love and the reflective age  **Lec:** 19  **Type:** critique  **Id:** 7477f702
**A:** Key:
1. False: love is not devoid of the sensuous, and the sensuous is not lust.
2. First love is "not simply sensual", and marriage is "sensual yet spiritual"; lust is the sensual on its own, without the stamp of the eternal.

This is step 3 of Kraal's diagram. B does rank the spiritual higher: the higher "the heaven over the marriage bed", the more aesthetic the marriage. But marriage keeps the sensual, now in "a yet deeper opposition" to the spiritual. B's reply to A about the altar makes the same point: "flesh is not sensuality, it is selfishness".

### Q: What does the civil-marriage note on Kraal's diagram say, and which step does it point at?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: the ethical as the more aesthetic  **Lec:** 19  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 3b0336bd
**A:** Parts:
1. A civil marriage, one without religious officials, does not "require love".
2. It points at step 1: the defining characteristic of marriage is love.

The note is the lecture's, not B's. B's nearest words are "a civil arrangement" for the reflective age's marriage for a period, ended by notifying the authority the way one gives notice of a change of address; such unions, he says, "are not marriages". For B a shared life without love is only the satisfaction of sensual desire or "an association, a partnership". The Church does not ask whether the couple love each other either, but because it "takes it for granted".

### Q: On Kraal's diagram, romantic love "will seek to transform into conjugal love". What is conjugal love, and why does romantic love seek it?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: the ethical as the more aesthetic  **Lec:** 19  **Type:** derive  **Id:** 2a561807
**A:** Key:
1. Conjugal love is married love.
2. Romantic love already claims eternity, but on its own its eternity is "illusory", with no inner history; the true eternity needs "a determination of the will", the intention and vow of marriage, so romantic love must become married love to be what it claims.

B says romantic love, being non-historical, "goes astray or comes to a standstill". He would not call the change a replacement: first love enters marriage "without being changed", and his word for the result is "transfiguration", the same love kept and made more glorious.

### Q: Which philosopher do some readers say Judge William (B) follows, and what in his letter supports it?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: the ethical as the more aesthetic  **Lec:** 19  **Type:** recall  **Id:** d1713bd4
**A:** Key:
1. Hegel: some argue that B is a Hegelian.
2. His Hegelian vocabulary, for example first love as "an unreal An-sich" (the in-itself) and marriage as "the immediacy which has mediacy in itself", and his joke that A begs him to keep away from "any attempt at mediation".

The lecture mentioned this as an aside. It is a reading of B, and B does not name Hegel in the letter. Hegel's *Philosophy of Right* (1820) makes marriage the first ethical relationship, love given a lasting and lawful form, which is close to B's case. Against the label, B is alarmed by the young men who cry "de omnibus dubitandum" (doubt everything), a slogan of the Hegelian philosophy then in fashion in Copenhagen.

### Q: Multiple choice. According to the lecture, B's argument that the ethical is the more aesthetic goes back to which work? (a) Plato's *Republic* (b) Plato's *Symposium* (c) Hegel's *Philosophy of Right* (d) Aristotle's *Nicomachean Ethics*
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: the ethical as the more aesthetic  **Lec:** 19  **Type:** recall  **Id:** ba1b02de
**A:** Key:
1. (a) Plato's *Republic*.

The principle Kraal took from it is that the beautiful derives its value from the good. The *Symposium* is Plato's dialogue on love, which makes (b) the tempting wrong answer. The *Philosophy of Right* is the Hegel book whose account of marriage is close to B's, one reason some readers call B a Hegelian.

## Reading: "The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage" (Nov 2–6; read by Claude 2026-11-02)

### Q: What are B's three criteria of romantic love?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: first love and the reflective age  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** recall  **Id:** c191fc17
**A:** Parts:
1. It is immediate: "to see her and to love her were one and the same".
2. It rests on natural necessity and on beauty.
3. It carries the consciousness of the eternal, which gives it nobility and separates it from lust.

The beauty is sensual beauty and the beauty that shows through the sensual. B says the old romances end where they should begin, with the lovers united in the fifth act, and that their struggle is entirely external; today money has replaced cruel fathers, with a rich uncle dying in the fifth act.

### Q: The reflective age, B says, struck out on two paths against romantic love. Name both and give his verdict on each.
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: first love and the reflective age  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 34cb071d
**A:** Parts:
1. The immoral path: keep the sensual and drop the eternal, in "the eternal instant of the embrace" and marriages for a period with a way out.
2. The respectable path: the marriage of convenience, really "the marriage of reason", a capitulation to life.
3. Verdict on the first: absolutely immoral, though as "a formal protest against marriage" it brings thought a step nearer the goal.
4. Verdict on the second: as immoral as it is unaesthetic, because a calculation is always temporal and so lacks the eternal.

Byron's "love is heaven, marriage hell" belongs to the first path, whose marriages end the way one gives notice of a change of address, and whose new definition of unhappy love is to be loved when one is no longer in love. The marriage of reason is entered by people who have learnt that love is an illusion; its only solace is despair.

### Q: Distinguish egoistic from sympathetic melancholy in B's first letter, and say why he thinks the age retreats from marriage.
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: first love and the reflective age  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 24c3627e
**A:** Parts:
1. Egoistic melancholy fears for itself: perhaps fate will tie me to another who is truly the ideal.
2. Sympathetic melancholy, nobler and more painful, fears for itself for the other's sake: perhaps what the beloved finds captivating in me will be taken from me.
3. The age neutralizes marriage out of cowardice and self-indulgence, not, as the Middle Ages did, because the unmarried life was thought more perfect.

Both kinds treat every day as "examination day". B calls melancholy "the defect of our time" and says marriages contracted for a period only sap the energy of married life. The trap is the second letter, where melancholy is "hysteria of the spirit", a different account.

### Q: Why, for B, must conjugal love not begin with doubt, and what is the "higher concentric immediacy" that first love is taken up into?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: first love and the reflective age  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** recall  **Id:** cf5e12e3
**A:** Key:
1. A marriage built on doubt would annihilate first love and so divorce marriage from love.
2. The higher concentric immediacy is the religious, "where rational reflection ends": first love is taken up into it unchanged, which enriches it rather than impoverishing it.

There love finds the infinity that reflective love sought in vain. B wants no rift in the ethical like the one between faith and knowledge in the intellectual sphere.

### Q: What is the substance of marriage for B, and what does marriage presuppose? Whom does he call the traitors of marriage?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: first love and the reflective age  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** recall  **Id:** a3a23b24
**A:** Parts:
1. The substance of marriage is love, "the being in love"; without it a shared life is sensual satisfaction or a partnership.
2. Marriage does not evoke love; it presupposes it "as something present, not past".
3. The traitors are not the seducers or the divorced but the husbands who rebel only in thought, "like lunatics each in his matrimonial cell", sighing for the engagement.

Marriage has an ethical and religious factor and rests on resignation; being in love does not. People who marry first and expect love to follow are "dove-fanciers" shutting two doves in a cage. If the thought-rebels were right, love would belong to the engagement and no one should marry.

### Q: Which Mozart figure does B use, for what, and how does first love differ from it?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: first love and the reflective age  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** apply  **Id:** b83e092f
**A:** Parts:
1. The figure is the Page in *Figaro*, Cherubino.
2. B uses him for the state before first love: "love makes blind", a "dreaming yet searching state".
3. First love by contrast is "an absolute wakefulness", aimed at "a single, definite, actual object" which is all there is for it.

B is reusing A's own example from "The Musical Erotic" but places it before first love rather than as a stage of it. The philistine who looks for a partner in the newspaper has excluded himself from first love altogether.

### Q: List the features B gives first love.
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: first love and the reflective age  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** recall  **Id:** f43580cc
**A:** Parts:
1. Absolute wakefulness, directed at one definite living being.
2. Sensual but not simply sensual.
3. It presupposes itself back into all eternity and forward into all eternity.
4. It is "the unity of freedom and necessity".
5. It is the unity of "the universal and the singular".

It is not simply sensual because the sensual as such comes forth only in reflection, and first love has no reflection. It has all of this immediately. The two are drawn to each other by an irresistible power and feel their freedom in it. B offers himself as "the noble father" who lets them come together "in the name of God".

### Q: How, for B, do the lovers relate their first love to God without changing it, and what does that do to the love?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: first love and the reflective age  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** recall  **Id:** e930d091
**A:** Key:
1. They thank God for their love; no reflection joins, and the love is taken up into "a higher concentricity".
2. The thanksgiving carries an intention, to hold on to the love, and that lifts first love into the ethical.

B's image is a swimming belt worn by someone who has never been in mortal danger. Thanking God also changes the man: instead of feeling he has conquered the girl, he humbles himself, and "it is in truth far more beautiful to take the beloved as a gift from the hand of God than to have subdued the whole world to conquer her". Possession becomes "daily possession throughout a whole lifetime".

### Q: Give A's three offences at the altar and B's reply to each.
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: first love and the reflective age  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 1f816b1e
**A:** Parts:
1. He is solemnly made her master: B says he already is, and his coquetry about wanting to be her slave is idolatry.
2. His beloved is declared a sinner: the Church proclaims sin only as woman's lot in general, and woman is "meek" and "much nearer to God than man".
3. A third power binds them to fidelity: it does not impose itself, the religiously developed seek it, and lovers swear by the moon and the stars anyway.

A's wider objection is that the Christian God is spirit and negates beauty and the sensual. B answers that "flesh is not sensuality, it is selfishness", so even the spiritual can be carnal, that the innocent sensual can be taken into Christianity, and that Scripture has "no special blessing for bachelors".

### Q: What does B make of the saying "one loves only once", and what is preferential love?
**Topic:** The Aesthetic Validity of Marriage: first love and the reflective age  **Lec:** 18  **Type:** recall  **Id:** 41f07224
**A:** Parts:
